# 0.1.9 work in progress (branch `sources-langs-0.1.9`)

Started Oct 2, 2026 while 0.1.8 finishes on `rust-first`. Plan: `specs/lazaret-sources-and-languages-plan-2026-10-02.md` (project docs).

## Done

- **Go lexer** (`rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/lex/go.rs`): `//` and `/* */` comments (no nesting), interpreted strings and runes, raw strings, numbers (hex floats, `_`, imaginary), Unicode names. An interpreted string or rune not closed on its line ends there; an unclosed comment or raw string runs to the end.
- **Rust lexer** (`rust/.../lex/rs.rs`): nested block comments, doc comments, strings that span lines, raw strings (`r#"…"#`), byte and C strings, characters and bytes told apart from lifetimes and loop labels, raw identifiers, numbers (`1..2`, `1.max(2)`, `1.5e-3f64`), a first-line shebang. Unclosed strings and comments run to the end of the text.
- `lex::structure(text, "go" | "rs", _)` returns comments, strings and literals, like the JavaScript and Python lexers.
- Tests: `lex/tests_go_rs.rs`, in a file of their own so `lex/tests.rs` is untouched.
- **Fuzzer for both lexers** (backlog item 4's first piece): `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/examples/fuzz_lex/` (`harness.rs`, `main.rs`), no external crates (CI's `check_rust_deps.py` allows none, so no libFuzzer). It builds programs from known tokens and mutates them, then checks: the program lexes back to exactly its tokens; cut at any token's start, the lexer returns the tokens before it; no panic, tokens in order, nothing outside a token but white space; a comment's or string's own text lexes to that one token; deterministic; `lex::structure` spans in order with no comment inside a literal; and linear time (200,000 characters of each opener that never closes). A failure prints its seed and iteration (`--seed S --iter I` reruns only it) and the smallest input that still fails.
  - Short gate: `lex/tests_fuzz.rs` (3 seeds x 1,500 programs per language, about a second), run by `cargo test` in CI's rust job.
  - Long runs: `cargo run --release --example fuzz_lex -- all --iters 300000 --seconds 60`. 300,000 programs and 900,000 mutants per language (41 and 54 million characters) found nothing past the bug below.
  - **First run found a real bug**: `'\'` repeated was quadratic in the Rust lexer (5 s for 200,000 characters), since a character literal looked for its line's end from every quote. Fixed (`quoted_line`: it stops at a line break as it reads).
  - Checked that it catches bugs: three injected ones (block comments that stop nesting, a raw string closing one `#` short, Go escapes not skipped) each failed at iteration 0.

- **P-3: `lazaret guard --from-plan pip install ...`** (`registry/guard.py`, tests in `tests/registry/test_guard_from_plan.py`). Opt-in. After pip's plan (`--dry-run --report`) is scanned, pip installs the scanned wheels from a folder (`--no-index --find-links`) instead of resolving and downloading a second time. On the profile's set (`requests boto3 pydantic`, `--target`, 2 vCPUs, cold verdict and pip caches, three runs each) the guarded install took 19.2 s at the median and 13.5 s with the flag; plain pip took 6.1 s. The trees `--target` receives are identical, byte for byte, with and without the flag.
  - **Why a folder and not `--no-deps --require-hashes` pins** (the backlog's first idea): pins would make pip mark every package as requested (the `REQUESTED` marker) and would need every pip option re-read. With the folder pip gets the user's own arguments (`-r`, `-c`, extras, `--target`, `--user`), so its own rules apply.
  - **Fails closed on the hash:** each file in the folder is hashed again just before pip runs and must be the one that was scanned; a file that is not is blocked, and `--trust` does not let it through. Pip then reads the file once more, so a local process running as the same user could still race that window.
  - **Falls back to the index** (with a line saying why) when the plan has a source distribution (it builds with requirements the index serves), a file too large to scan, an empty plan, or a folder that can't be made. uv is not covered (its second run is quick); other tools ignore the flag with a note.
  - **Left for later:** the remaining 13.5 s is mostly the plan itself (pip downloads and resolves through the index while it dry-runs): P-15's connection reuse and scanning while fetching are the next levers. For the release: CHANGELOG entry, README and `docs/` text for `--from-plan` (0.1.8 agent's files until the tag); decide then whether it becomes the default.

- **S-2, offline half: workflow hardening checks** (`scanner/ghworkflow.py`: `hardening(text)` and `hardening_rule(kind, detail)`; npm twin `js/src/lib/ghworkflow.js`; tests `tests/scanner/test_ghworkflow_hardening.py` and `tests/architecture/test_js_parity_workflow_hardening.py`). `hardening` returns `(kind, line, detail)` by line; `hardening_rule` gives the rule (`id, name, type, sev, msg, why, fix, ref`) that `core.mk_issue` takes. Seven kinds, six rule ids (the seventh, `pipe-to-shell`, was added with S-3, which shares it):
  - `unpinned` -> `SC-WORKFLOW-UNPINNED`: a `uses:` of an action, a reusable workflow or an image that is not a full commit SHA or a digest. MAJOR; MINOR for GitHub's own actions (`actions/`, `github/`) at a version tag.
  - `pr-checkout` -> `SC-WORKFLOW-PR-CHECKOUT` (CRITICAL): a `pull_request_target` job that checks out the pull request: `actions/checkout` with a `ref:` or `repository:` taken from the PR head, `git checkout/fetch/switch/pull/merge/cherry-pick` with a PR ref, and any `gh pr checkout`.
  - `cache` -> `SC-WORKFLOW-CACHE`: a cache restored in a release workflow (event `release`, an OIDC grant, a publish action or a publish command). MAJOR when asked for (`actions/cache`, `cache: npm`), MINOR when a setup action does it by default.
  - `perms` and `perms-missing` -> `SC-WORKFLOW-PERMISSIONS`: a write scope (or `write-all`) for every job (MAJOR); no `permissions:` at the top and a job with none of its own (MINOR).
  - `oidc-install` -> `SC-WORKFLOW-OIDC-INSTALL` (MAJOR): a job that can request an OIDC token (its own `permissions:`, or the workflow's when it has none) and installs dependencies without `--ignore-scripts`.
  - `pipe-to-shell` -> `SC-WORKFLOW-PIPE-SHELL` (MAJOR): a script that fetches a script and runs it unread: `curl … | sh` (also through `sudo`, `env`, `VAR=x`, `tee`, `|&`), `bash <(curl …)`, `sh -c "$(curl …)"`, `eval "$(wget …)"`, `iwr … | iex`. The fetch counts only where a command starts (`echo curl x | sh` is not one); an interpreter other than a shell counts only when it reads standard input (`| python3 -`, not `| python3 -m json.tool`); `sh -c "$(…)"` counts, `sh $(…)` does not. Continued lines (`… \`) are joined first (`ghworkflow.logical_lines`). The helpers `install_command`, `publish_command`, `pipe_to_shell`, `logical_lines` and `records(text, items, seq_blocks)` are public: `gitlabci` uses them.
  - **A bug found on the way, in the first S-2 commit:** a workflow with Windows line ends (CRLF) left a `\r` on every value, so a pinned action read as unpinned (`…@<sha>\r` is 41 characters) and `id-token: write\r` was not a write grant. The outline now drops a line's `\r` (both twins); the parity corpus includes CRLF files. Commands also start after `if`, `elif`, `while` and `until` now.
  - **`findings()` is untouched** (its `secrets` and `backdoor` kinds): `core.workflow_issues` and `scan.js workflowIssues` send any other kind to the backdoor branch, so the new kinds are reported apart and wired in by S-4 (wire-up item 6).
  - **Commands are read where a command starts** (line start, after `; & | (`, `then/do/else/sudo/time/exec/command`, after `VAR=value` words), so `echo npm ci` and a commit message that names it are not installs.
  - **Linear time**: the first version was quadratic in the number of jobs (4,000 jobs: 11.7 s); permissions are now read in one pass and steps grouped by job (16,000 jobs, 2 MB: 0.56 s in Python, 0.2 s in JS). Adversarial lines (`;a=b` x 50,000, 200,000 spaces after `git`, ...) are in the tests.
  - Checked: the Python and JS results and rules are equal on 1,500 generated workflows plus the curated ones (the pattern text and lists are compared too); eight injected twin bugs were each caught; 60 unit tests (with `pipe-to-shell`, CRLF and continued lines); the existing persistence tests (outline and findings) unchanged. For `pipe-to-shell` 25 more bugs were injected: 4 survived at first, tests were added, all are caught.
  - **Not covered**: `workflow_run` checkouts, script injection through `env:`, YAML anchors, what a repository's default token permission is (so `perms-missing` stays MINOR).
  - **Left for the online half**: `registry/actions.py` (resolve each `uses:` at its pinned SHA through `sources.py`: moved tag, impostor commit, tag off the default branch).

- **S-2, online half: what a workflow's `uses:` really point to** (`registry/actions.py`; `ghworkflow.uses()` and `parse_uses()` give it the lines; tests `tests/registry/test_actions.py` against an in-memory GitHub, `tests/registry/_actions_support.py`, which S-1 and S-4 can reuse). Python only: it is a registry module, there is no npm twin. `python -m lazaret.registry.actions [--no-pins | --pins FILE] [--accept-moved] [--max-calls N] WORKFLOW...` prints the report (exit 1 a finding, 3 something unchecked, 2 usage). Six kinds, six rule ids (`actions.rule(kind, detail)` builds the rule as `hardening_rule` does):
  - `impostor` -> `SC-ACTION-IMPOSTOR`: a pin that is in no branch and at no tag tip of the repository (GitHub resolves a fork's commit under the parent's name). CRITICAL when every tag tip and every branch was compared, MAJOR when the repository has more than 100 tags or branches, or more than 9 branches (the default branch, then release branches, are compared first), and the message says what was not looked at.
  - `tag-moved` -> `SC-ACTION-TAG-MOVED` (CRITICAL): a version tag (`v4.1.0`, not `v4`) that points to another commit than when it was first seen. What was seen is kept in the pin book (`~/.cache/lazaret/actions-pins.json`, `LAZARET_ACTIONS_PINS`, `--pins`): the first sighting is no finding, a moved tag keeps its first entry so the alert stays until `--accept-moved`, and a missing book only finds less. Floating tags are left alone.
  - `off-branch` -> `SC-ACTION-OFF-BRANCH` (MAJOR): a tag whose commit is in none of the branches compared.
  - `pin-mismatch` -> `SC-ACTION-PIN-MISMATCH` (MAJOR): `@<sha> # v4.1.0` where v4.1.0 points elsewhere or is no tag (comments `# v1.2.3`, `# tag=v1.2.3`, `# ratchet:o/r@v1.2.3`; a floating `# v4` says nothing).
  - `docker-unpinned` -> `SC-ACTION-DOCKER-UNPINNED` (MAJOR) and `nested-unpinned` -> `SC-ACTION-NESTED-UNPINNED`: read from the action's `action.yml` at the commit (a Docker image without a digest; a composite action's steps that are not pinned, and those looked at in turn, two levels deep, with the chain in `via`).
  - **Says what it did not check**: a GitHub refusal (the rate limit: 60 an hour without `GITHUB_TOKEN`), the call budget (45 without a token, 400 with), an expression in a ref, a path that cannot go in a URL, a repository it cannot see are `incomplete`, so the action is not cleared; the run stops asking after a rate-limit answer. A pinned release costs two calls (tag list, `action.yml`); an untagged pin about five.
  - **Rules of `sources.py`**: HTTPS to `api.github.com` only, names from the workflow validated before they are put in a URL, token only to the API host and in no report, bounded responses.
  - Checked: 54 tests; 20 injected bugs, each caught. Against the real API (read through a web fetch, since the sandbox has no GitHub credentials): the tag list's shape and `compare`'s `status` ("behind" for a release commit against main) are as the code reads them; `git/ref/tags/...` answered 403 through that route, so its shape is the documented one and untested live. **A live run with a token is the check still to make.**
  - **Not covered**: scanning the action's own code (a JavaScript `main`, `pre` and `post` like an npm package, a composite's `run:` as shell: that needs `sources.checkout` and the engine, so S-1/S-4), a Dockerfile's `FROM` lines, GitHub Enterprise Server, nested `uses:` comments, the content of a reusable workflow.
  - **For S-4/S-1**: the CLI can print `rules` for each finding; the six `SC-ACTION-*` ids need README, `docs/DESIGN.md` and CHANGELOG lines, and a network scan reports them only when the user asked for the online checks.

- **S-3: GitLab CI checks** (`scanner/gitlabci.py`: `hardening(text)`, `hardening_rule(kind, detail)`, `is_gitlab_ci(path)`, `mr_text(line)`; npm twin `js/src/lib/gitlabci.js`; tests `tests/scanner/test_gitlabci.py` (58) and `tests/architecture/test_js_parity_gitlabci.py`). The same shape as the GitHub checks and kept apart from `core`: wiring is S-4's (`is_gitlab_ci` is `.gitlab-ci.yml`, `*.gitlab-ci.yml` and any .yml/.yaml under a `.gitlab` directory). It reads the outline of the YAML like `ghworkflow` does (`records(..., seq_blocks=True)`, so `- |` in a script list is a block). Seven kinds, five rule ids:
  - `include-remote`, `include-project`, `include-component` -> `SC-GITLAB-INCLUDE`: a file from a URL (MAJOR; CRITICAL over http); a project's file at no ref, a branch or a tag instead of a full commit SHA (`ref: $CI_COMMIT_SHA` counts as pinned; MINOR at a version tag); a component at `~latest`, a branch, a partial version or none (MAJOR) or at an exact `1.2.3` (MINOR), not at a SHA. Block lists, a single map, a string, and flow lists and maps (`include: [{remote: …}]`) are read.
  - `image` -> `SC-GITLAB-IMAGE`: `image:` (string, `name:` map, flow map) and `services:` without a `@sha256:` digest, in a job, in `default:` and at the top. MINOR for an official image at a version (`python:3.12`), MAJOR for `latest`, no tag or any other registry. An image under `$CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE` (one the project builds) is left alone.
  - `pipe-to-shell` -> `SC-GITLAB-PIPE-SHELL`: the shared check above, on `before_script`, `script`, `after_script`, `default:`, top-level scripts and lists under hidden keys (`.setup: &setup` + `- curl … | sh`), one per job.
  - `mr-text` -> `SC-GITLAB-MR-TEXT` (MAJOR): a merge request's title, description, labels or source branch, a commit's message, title, description or tag message, or a ref or branch name (`$CI_MERGE_REQUEST_TITLE`, `$CI_COMMIT_BRANCH`, …) in `eval`, in the argument of `sh -c`, or in the place of a command. **Not** a bare `echo "$CI_COMMIT_TITLE"`: GitLab gives a variable as environment text, and only code that reads it again (`eval`, `sh -c "…$VAR…"` in double quotes) turns a title containing `$(…)` into commands; inside single quotes it stays data.
  - `token-install` -> `SC-GITLAB-TOKEN-INSTALL` (MAJOR): a job with a publishing credential (`id_tokens:`, its own or the default's; `secrets:`; a registry token variable such as `$NPM_TOKEN`, `$PYPI_*TOKEN`, `$TWINE_PASSWORD`, `$CI_JOB_JWT_V2`) that installs dependencies without `--ignore-scripts`; a job without its own `before_script` gets the default's.
  - **Linear time**: hostile files are in both engines' tests (50,000 includes, `bash -c "` x 50,000, 50,000 continued lines, …). The first JS run took 7.9 s on 50,000 continued lines (a string concatenated and searched on each line); `logical_lines` now collects parts (both twins).
  - Checked: the Python and JS results and rules are equal on 1,200 generated pipelines and the curated ones; the outline with block scalars in lists, `mr_text`, `parse_image`, `flow_items` and `is_gitlab_ci` are compared on their own; the pattern text is compared; 59 bugs were injected into the module: 10 survived at first (a non-hidden list read as a script, a single-map `include:` with several keys, an unbalanced bracket in a flow list, …), tests were added, and all are caught.
  - **Not covered**: `extends:`, `!reference` and anchors are not resolved (a script is read where it is written, so an anchored list is read under its hidden key); a `rules:`-controlled include; the content of a remote or project include (S-1 can fetch it); `trigger:` child pipelines; a Dockerfile's `FROM`; variable expansion in an image name; a self-hosted runner's tags (the GitHub `backdoor` check has its self-hosted shape, GitLab's has none yet).
  - **For S-4**: both modules need a loop in `core.py` and `scan.js` (`for kind, line, d in gitlabci.hardening(text)` for a file where `is_gitlab_ci(path)`), the five `SC-GITLAB-*` ids in README, `docs/DESIGN.md` and CHANGELOG, and the identical-output check excludes them.
- **S-1: `lazaret [scan] github:owner/repo[@ref]` and `gitlab:group/project[@ref]`** (`registry/sourcescan.py`; `_cli.py` hands a command line with a `github:` or `gitlab:` argument to it; tests `tests/registry/test_sourcescan.py`, 41). `sources.checkout` resolves the ref to a commit and reads its archive into a directory; `sourcescan` runs the ordinary scan (`core.main`, every option) on it and removes the directory. `scan` as the first word is optional. Python only (the npm CLI has no sources). What it adds to a scan of a folder:
  - **The commit is named**: a `Source: github:o/r@<sha> (the ref v1)` line before the report, and report files named for it (`lazaret-github-o-r-0123456-report.json` and `.html`) in the current directory. The scan root is a temporary directory, so core's default (under the root) would lose them; `--out-dir`, `--json`, `--html` and `--sarif` still decide (the defaults are put in front of the user's options, so argparse's last-one wins), and the destination is checked before anything is fetched (exit 3, no request made). When every wanted report has an absolute path, `--out-dir` is not asked about.
  - **Fail closed on an incomplete read.** A path the archive left out (`export-ignore`), a tree too large to list, an archive that spent a budget, a file that could not be written: said on stderr after the report, and with `--ci` the exit status is 1 even when the gate passed. `--accept-incomplete` scans what was read and exits by the gate alone (and says so). Without `--ci` the exit status is the scan's, as for SC-TRUNCATED. A file only too large to read (`repo.MAX_MEMBER`) is a warning, not a gap: a scan of a folder does not read one either. This will fail `--ci` on every repository that uses `export-ignore` for its tests and `.github` (most PHP libraries do); see "Not covered".
  - **Options are read as argparse reads them**: `--out`, `--c`, `--json=x`, a value that starts with `github:` (`--exclude github:x/y`) and a folder named `github:o/r` are not mistaken for the source; a second thing to scan, or `--help` / `--version`, is a usage error or goes to core with nothing fetched. The option table in `sourcescan.py` is held to `core.main --help` by a test (a new option in core that is missing there fails it).
  - Exit codes are the scan's; a spec that is not one, or a repository that cannot be fetched, is 2 (an unreadable target); a bug while fetching is `error: internal`, exit 5. The token and `LAZARET_GITLAB_URL` are read from the environment by `sources.py` only; the token is in no output (tested).
  - Checked: 41 tests (the argument reading, the destination, the fail-closed table, the cleanup, the real scan of a two-file repository and its report, which needs the native engine and is skipped without it); the layering test; 205 AST mutants (comparisons swapped, and/or, forced conditions, constants moved, returns and calls removed): 192 are caught. 43 survived the first run (some falsely: a stale `.pyc` hid same-size mutants, so the mutator now writes no bytecode); tests were added for the equals-sign value, a missing value, the exact count of lines shown, the defaults taken from the process, and a long path's name. The 13 left are equivalent: argparse's prefix match covers the exact-name test, `scan` placement is refused above, the `__main__` guard, `configure_stdio`, `rmtree(ignore_errors)`.
  - **N-5 (S-4, part 4): the `export-ignore` paths are fetched, and the report carries the source.** `sources.checkout` lists the commit's tree (as before) and fetches each path the archive lacks on its own (`Client.file`): a public GitHub repository's from `raw.githubusercontent.com` by the commit, outside the API's 60 requests an hour and with no token sent there; with a token through the API's contents call (`application/vnd.github.raw`); on GitLab through the repository files call. A file is written only when it is the blob the tree names (`blob N\0` + bytes, SHA-1, or SHA-256 for a 64-digit id); one that is not is an archive anomaly and stays missing. At most `MAX_MISSING_FILES` (300) requests within `MISSING_FILES_SECONDS` (the registry's scan budget) and the download budget; a rate limit stops the asking; a file over the size limit is skipped as in the archive; a path that would leave the checkout is not written. What is still missing makes the checkout incomplete, as before, with the reason. The scan is then given a `source` block (`sourcescan.source_block`; `core.main(argv, source=…)`, `core.set_source`): the report's `project` is `github:o/r@<sha>` instead of the temporary path, `source` has the spec, kind, repository, the ref asked for (null: the default branch), the commit, its address (`https://github.com/o/r`, or the GitLab instance's), files, bytes, `complete`, `incomplete`, `skipped`, `notes` and `anomalies`, and no token or temporary path; a checkout not read whole sets the result's `incomplete` and `incompleteReason`, as an MCP scan that stopped early does. The SARIF report gains `versionControlProvenance` (repository, commit, mapped to `%SRCROOT%`). Tests: `tests/registry/test_sources_missing.py` (9) and `test_sourcescan.py`'s `SourceBlock` and end-to-end additions (5).
  - **Not covered** (later): `--help` of core does not list `--accept-incomplete` or the `github:` form (core's parser); Actions' own code (`uses:` at pinned SHAs read as composite shell, `main`, `pre`, `post`) through `checkout` (N-4); the contents of a GitLab remote or project `include:`; submodules (named in a note, not read); the npm CLI has no sources.
- **P-4: the timing helper, and `guard --timings`** (`scanner/timings.py`; tests `tests/scanner/test_timings.py`, 39, and `tests/registry/test_guard_timings.py`, 18). `with timings.span("engine", "scan_file"):` records seconds and a call under a phase and a name when a capture is open (`with timings.capture() as t, t.run():`), and costs one global read and a shared no-op object when none is.
  - **Exclusive time**: a span's seconds are its own, without the spans inside it, so a call site added later inside an existing span moves seconds to where they belong and the outer one shrinks by exactly that. Threads keep their own stacks and their phases add up (so they can exceed the wall; the table says so); `other` is the wall minus what the thread that made the `Timings` spent in spans. A worker process makes its own, returns `report()` with its answer and the parent calls `merge()`. A phase keeps 64 names, then `(other names)`. The report is plain numbers and names (`{"wall", "other", "phases": {phase: {"seconds", "calls", "by": {name: {...}}}}}`); `render()` is the stderr table.
  - **`lazaret guard --timings`** prints the table on stderr when the run ends and puts the report in `--json` (`timings`). Spans: `network` (`request`: connect, TLS and waiting for the headers, which urllib does not split; `read`), `scan` (`artifact`: `repo._scan_artifact`, in the worker, merged back; `wait for worker`: the parent's wait, so `wait - artifact` is the hand-off and queueing that P-7 is about), `tool` (`pip resolve` for a capture run, `pip run` for the install). A worker is asked for its report only when the run keeps timings (an extra `timed` argument to `_scan_one`; the answer's `timings` key is taken out before the verdict goes on). A refused command line prints no table.
  - **A first real run** (`lazaret guard --timings --jobs 2 pip install --target … requests`, this sandbox, whose network goes through a proxy): wall 8.33 s: `tool` 7.46 (`pip run` 4.54, `pip resolve` 2.92), `network` 3.39 summed over six threads (25 requests: `request` 3.34, `read` 0.05), `scan` 1.10 (`wait for worker` 0.65, `artifact` 0.45, five artifacts), `other` 0.87.
  - **For the 0.1.8 agent (P-4 call sites, `repo.py`)**: `from lazaret.scanner import timings`, then `with timings.span("network", "fetch"):` around `_fetch`; `("archive", "iter_archive")` around the archive reader; `("engine", "<call name>")` around each `engine.*` call (`scan_file`, `import_time_risk`, `agent_hijack`, `spawned_scripts`, `cross_file`), and the guard's outer `scan` span stays as it is: its own seconds shrink by what these take. For the registry CLI: `--timings` as in `guard.main` (capture, `run()`, `render()` on stderr, the report in `--json`); a pool of workers returns `report()` and the parent merges it, as `guard._scan_one` does.
  - Checked: the 39 and 18 tests; 116 AST mutants of `timings.py` (110 caught; the 6 left return values of context managers, a constant, and a float boundary) and the guard lines that changed (all caught but one equivalent default). Not covered: `repo.py`'s call sites and the registry CLI's flag (above); the guard's index relay (`network/relay`) is timed but its streaming to pip is in the same span.

- **P-15: connections that stay open between a guarded install's requests** (`registry/keepalive.py`, `guard.Fetcher`; tests `tests/registry/test_keepalive.py`, 89). Opt-in: `lazaret guard --keepalive pip install …`, off by default.
  - **Why:** `urllib` sends `Connection: close`, so each of the 25 to 50 requests of an install (index pages, `.metadata` files, the files) opens a TCP connection and a TLS session, where pip's own client reuses them: 48 connections for 48 requests in the profile's pip plan, 0.5 s of connect time summed here.
  - **The pool** (`keepalive.Pool`: thread-safe `http.client` connections; `Pool.request` is one GET that follows no redirect and adds no header). A connection goes back only when the body was read to its end and the server did not say `Connection: close` (an HTTP/1.0 answer without keep-alive and a body that ends with the connection count as closing); a response closed early closes its connection. A stale reused connection (`RemoteDisconnected`, a reset, a broken pipe, a TLS EOF) is dropped with its idle siblings and the GET is sent once more on a new connection; a new connection that fails is the caller's error. 30 s idle, 8 per (scheme, host, port, proxy). TLS is `ssl.create_default_context()`. A proxy from the environment is used for https as `urllib` uses it (one CONNECT through an `http://` proxy, `Proxy-Authorization` from the URL); any other proxy raises `Unsupported` and the fetch goes the `urllib` way as before; loopback is never proxied.
  - **What stays the guard's:** `Fetcher._open_kept` follows redirects by the rules of `_opener`: at most `repo.MAX_REDIRECTS`, `urllib`'s repeat rule (the fifth sight of a URL is refused; a test counts the requests on both paths), every target through `check`, a hop carrying only the credentials of its own URL. An answer that is neither 2xx nor a redirect is the same `FetchError` with `.status` and the same hints (`Fetcher._http_error`). `Fetcher.close()` closes the pool; the npm lockfile check, the uv check and `LocalIndex.close` call it (tested, also when a check raises). Connecting is a `network`/`connect` span and each answer is counted under `connections` as `new` or `reused`, so `--timings` shows what reuse did.
  - **A bug found on the way, fixed on both paths:** the guard accepted a body shorter than its `Content-Length` without an error (`HTTPResponse.read(n)` returns what arrived), so a cut-off index page read as a shorter page. It is now an `incomplete response` `FetchError`.
  - **The gain is not shown.** The sandbox's network goes through an agent proxy where a new connection costs about 28 ms: 12 PyPI pages took 0.33 s on new connections and 0.8 s on one kept connection through the proxy's tunnel, so the flag stays off. To measure on a direct network: `lazaret guard --timings [--keepalive] pip install …` (the `network` `request` lines and the `connections` counts) or `python scripts/profile/guard_connections.py`. If it pays, making it the default is a one-line change plus README and CHANGELOG lines (the 0.1.8 agent's files until the tag).
  - **Checked:** 89 tests (a local keep-alive server; TLS and a CONNECT proxy when `openssl` exists; HTTP/1.0, chunked, short, no-length and stale answers; the redirect, credential and host rules on both paths; resource tests that every connection ends idle or closed whichever way a request ends). On the device (Python 3.10) the same 89 pass, with the layering, portability and stdlib-only tests; the guard suites there fail the same tests with and without the change (its pip lacks `--dry-run`). AST mutants: `keepalive.py` 139 of 147 (`proxy_for` and `Response` 56 of 63, `Pool` 83 of 84; the 8 left are equivalent: a read size, a loop bound, the text of one message, a `close()` that `http.client` has already done); `guard.py`'s changed regions 102 of 110. The first run left 14 survivors in `Fetcher.fetch`, the redirect loop and the lockfile check, and tests were added for each real one (the declared-length check and its boundary, the budget for bodies with no length, the message's megabytes, `urllib`'s repeat count, the fetcher closed after the npm check). What is left is equivalent (`while True`, a read size, `exc.close()`, the bare-host `/` that the pool adds anyway) or not P-15's code (two uv source tests).
  - **Not covered:** `https://` proxies, HTTP/2, a proxy for a plain-http URL (the `urllib` path), pip's and npm's own downloads (they keep their own connections), overlapping downloads with scanning (P-9).
- **P-7: the guard's scans run in workers with limits, not in the guard's own process** (`registry/scanpool.py`, `guard.Scanner`; tests `tests/registry/test_scanpool.py`, 55, `test_guard_isolation.py`, 21, support module `_scanpool_support.py`). On by default; `--no-isolate` brings the old way back.
  - **Why:** with `--jobs 1` the scan ran in the process that downloads, holds every archive and talks to the package manager, and that process peaked at 2.15 GB on six large archives here (1.55 GB in the profile's install); and when a worker died the pool went on scanning in that same process. The hand-off also pickled the archive (copied three times, queued in the parent ahead of the worker).
  - **A file, not a pickle:** the parent writes the bytes to a directory of its own (mode 0700; a file named `<sha256>-<n>.bin`, mode 0600) and sends the worker the path. The worker reads the file and hashes it again: bytes that are not the ones staged make the scan fail (`ArchiveChanged`), never a verdict on other bytes. The file goes as soon as the answer is in, the directory when the pool closes. The digest in the name is what a seen-set shared by the workers (P-2a's guard half) or an in-flight table keyed by digest needs; neither is written.
  - **Limits that follow the work:** each worker sets once an address-space limit (`RLIMIT_AS`, default 6144 MB, `--worker-memory MB`, 0 for none; Linux only, ignored elsewhere), and for each archive the parent says how many engine threads it may use: the cores shared out among the archives running at that moment (all of them for one big archive alone, half each for two), so `--jobs` times engine threads stays within the cores. The worker then sets a CPU-time limit (`RLIMIT_CPU`) above the scan's own deadline as a backstop for a scan that ignores it. `engine.THREADS` is a module global the engine reads at each call; the pool is the only writer.
  - **What a lost worker means:** every archive in flight in that pool is run once more, alone, in a pool of its own. One that kills its worker again raises `Died`, and the guard blocks that package as "could not be checked" (`--trust` lets it through, as for any scan that fails). The work split said INCOMPLETE; the guard has no such verdict, and "blocked, not cleared" is what the guard does with a scan error, so that is what this is. A scan that does not answer within the wall limit (`2 * timeout + 60`) gets the pool's processes terminated and raises `Hung` ("the scan did not finish", as before). After `MAX_BREAKS` (20) lost workers the pool starts no more and raises `Died`, never scanning in the guard's own process.
  - **A pool that cannot start is told apart from one that loses a worker:** the first pool is proved by one trivial job. If that fails (no `spawn`, a limit too small to import Python, a frozen program), `Unavailable`, and the guard scans in its own process from then on and says so once (`lazaret guard: no scan worker could be started; scanning in this process`).
  - **Measured** (this sandbox: 2 vCPU; six cached archives, 28 to 41 MB, scanned from six threads as the guard's run_all does it; `/tmp` scripts `measure.py`, three runs of each pair, quiet machine):

    | | wall | the guard's own peak |
    |---|---|---|
    | before, `--jobs 2` (pickled) | 30.6 / 31.3 / 31.7 s | 272 MB (a worker: 1.3 GB) |
    | now, `--jobs 2` (file) | 31.2 / 29.9 / 30.8 s | 219 MB |
    | before, `--jobs 1` (in the guard's process) | 48.7 s | 2,154 MB |
    | now, `--jobs 1` (one worker) | 50.0 s | 219 MB |

    The wall time does not change (the earlier noisy runs went 46 to 57 s for the same pairs; the machine's noise is larger than the difference), and isolation at `--jobs 1` costs about 3% (the worker's start and the file). **What P-7 buys is the guard's memory and a scan that cannot take the guard down, not speed.** Not measured: scaling on four or more cores (P-7's done-when asks for it; there are two here), and macOS or Windows.
  - **Checked:** 76 tests. The pool: the hand-off (a file with a digest name, mode, removed after the answer and when the run fails, the re-hash), the limits as the worker sees them (`RLIMIT_AS`, `RLIMIT_CPU`, the engine's threads, with `jobs` sharing the cores), each way a worker ends (an exit, a crash, a spin killed by the CPU limit, a hang killed by the wall limit, an allocation over the memory limit, an exception), the retry rules (one archive alone, two that were in flight together, a worker lost on a retry, `MAX_BREAKS`), the pool that cannot start, `close()` while a worker is busy and while an archive waits for its retry, a lone big archive getting all the cores, a queued archive not counted against the wall limit, and the pool's own records with a stand-in for the executor (who is retired and who is killed, a lost worker counted once, the retry's pool tracked and ended by `close()`, the limits an archive gets, the staging folder). The guard: which `jobs` and `isolate` make workers, the options and their errors, the words of each failure, the one line about falling back, a real worker giving a real scan's answer on a real archive (needs the native engine). Python 3.13 here. On the device (Python 3.10) the scanpool, isolation and timings suites pass (the real-worker test skips there: no native engine), with the layering, stdlib-only and portability tests; the other guard suites fail the same tests with the change and without it (the device's pip lacks `--dry-run`, and it has no native engine). AST mutants of `scanpool.py`: 147 in the first full run, 121 killed. The 26 survivors were all the pool's own bookkeeping, which no test had looked at (a retired pool's processes not ended, a lost worker counted twice, the retry's pool not tracked for `close()`, the wall and CPU limits an archive gets, the staging folder, the file removed when a write fails); 17 tests went in for them (`BookkeepingTests`). The re-run on the changed regions (41 mutants, the whole module's tests) kills 39, and the two left are a 120-second constant for the start-up job (equivalent) and a second look for a pool another thread made (a test was added after the run: that region, 17 mutants, kills 15 with the bookkeeping tests and the other two with the rest).
  - **Not covered:** a memory or CPU limit on Windows (no `resource` module: the workers are replaceable and the wall limit still holds) and any test on macOS (`RLIMIT_AS` is set where the module has it, but how far the kernel enforces it was not tried), anything against a worker that reads the whole disk (it is a process of the same user), the shared seen-set and the in-flight table by digest (P-2a's guard half: the file names are ready for them), a pool per tool run rather than per process, README and CHANGELOG lines for `--no-isolate` and `--worker-memory` (the 0.1.8 agent's files until the tag).
- **P-5: an indexed CVE bundle, read only where a scan asks** (`scanner/sca_index.py`; `sca.py`, `sca_feeds.py`; tests `tests/scanner/test_sca_index.py`, 77; two fuzz targets; `scripts/profile/sca_bundle.py`). Opt-in: `lazaret-sca --update-bundle --bundle-format index --bundle cve-bundle.lzx`; `--bundle` takes either kind (told apart by the first eight bytes), and the JSON bundle stays the default and is unchanged.
  - **Why:** a scan parses the whole `cve-bundle.json` (about 92 MB) and builds its name index in memory to look up a few hundred names. On a synthetic bundle of the real one's size (120,000 advisories, 99.6 MB; `/tmp` generator, the sandbox cannot reach OSV): JSON load 6.8 s and 640 MB resident, then 0.05 s to match 2,000 dependencies (1,240 hits); the indexed file opens in 0.02 s and matches the same 2,000 in 0.2 s, 42 MB resident, **the same 1,240 matches**. 6.9 s becomes 0.22 s, and memory falls 15 times.
  - **The file:** a 104-byte header (magic, format 1, the file's length, counts, where each part is, CRC-32s), the metadata (JSON: `generatedAt`, `sources`, `counts`, the warnings counted when it was built, the feeds' own metadata), an advisory table, a key table sorted by a 16-byte digest of the name's key, then zlib records: one per advisory (the advisory as the document had it) and one per name (`[order, advisory, package]` triples). A key is `E NUL ecosystem NUL name` for an exact (package-manager) name and `L NUL name` for each lookup variant of a loose (CPE) name. A lookup bisects the table in memory, reads the name's record and each advisory's, and sorts the pairs by `order`, the package entry's position in the whole bundle: so `advisories_for` returns the pairs `CveBundle.advisories_for` does, in the same order. Ecosystems are only part of a key, so Go and crates.io advisories are more keys and no new format; what they need is `normalize_advisory`'s ecosystem list and `name_variants`/`exact_name_key` (G-1/R-1's SCA half).
  - **Nothing is re-implemented:** `sca.normalize_advisory` and `sca.bundle_header` are `CveBundle`'s own reading, pulled out as functions (no change of behaviour; the 48 + 61 + 32 + 19 + 15 + 25 + 11 existing SCA tests are unchanged and pass). The writer runs a document through them, so the warnings a build prints and the warnings a scan prints are the same lines, and a hostile advisory is cleaned the same way from either file (a test puts a document of wrong types through both).
  - **Fails closed, in every way a file can be wrong:** magic, format (a format it does not know says so and how to rebuild), the file's length against the header's (cut or extended), the header, metadata and both tables against their CRCs, the layout (the parts in the writer's order, no overlap), the key table strictly sorted (an unsorted table would miss a name and clear a dependency); then each record against its CRC, inflated within 8 MiB (a bomb is refused without being inflated: the test's 64 MB of zeros stays under 24 MB of memory), parsed with the bounded JSON reader, and checked to be the record it says it is; a posting that names an advisory or package that is not there. All of it is `BundleDamaged`, a `ValueError`, so `lazaret-sca` exits 4 as for any bundle it cannot read, **also when a record is found damaged in the middle of matching** (no report is written). A test cuts a file at every length and changes every byte of it: each is refused. CRCs find damage, not tampering; a bundle you do not trust can still say nothing is vulnerable, as a JSON one can.
  - **Bounded, as the build was:** `sca_feeds`' reading of the feeds (`read_osv_zip`, the zip preflight, `bounded_lines`, the record and row limits: the L1 fix) is untouched, and the new reader adds its own bounds (record, metadata, advisory and key counts, the caches at 20,000 each).
  - **Writing:** `write_bundle(doc, path, fmt="index")` writes to a temporary file, fsyncs, **reads the whole file back and checks every record against every other (`verify`: each name's entry sorted, listing only packages indexed under it, and every package listed under every name it has)**, and only then replaces the old bundle; a document the index cannot hold (NaN) or a failed check leaves the old bundle and no temporary file. The same build is deterministic (same document, same bytes). `python -m lazaret.scanner.sca_index build IN.json OUT | check FILE | info FILE` converts and checks a bundle.
  - **Fuzzed:** `sca-bundle-index` (damaged files, also with the length and every checksum made right so the checks behind the checksums run: only `ValueError`; a file that passes `verify` never fails a lookup; answers of the right shape; two reads agree) and `sca-bundle-doc` (a JSON document through `CveBundle` and through the index must answer alike for every name it mentions): 36,000 and 30,000 inputs, nothing found. Each promise is shown live by a reader that breaks it (`test_fuzz_scripts.py`, 69).
  - **Checked:** 77 tests: the equivalence on the fixture and on 60 random bundles (pairs, order, warnings, metadata, the sequence, `match_inventory`), reads counted (opening: four; an absent name: none; a present one: its record and its advisories, once), thread safety, the caches' bound, the closed bundle, the writer (determinism, summary against the JSON bundle's own indexes, what it refuses), every cut and changed byte, hostile files with right checksums (tables out of order, records that are not zlib, cut, followed by bytes, not JSON, too deep, another record's, postings that point nowhere, a bomb, metadata that lies), the CLI (the two formats give the same report; a damaged bundle is exit 4 before and during matching; closed after use). The layering, stdlib-only and portability tests pass. AST mutants of `sca_index.py`: 367 mutants, 354 killed, 13 survivors, each an equivalent mutant: the cache sizes and the zlib level (tuning numbers; a test cannot tell 20,000 from 20,001), the size of a read from the records and a loop whose test is already true, a default for a parameter nothing reads, a `seek` the previous read had already made, the advisory number the writer gives `normalize_advisory` (it only reaches a warning the writer discards) and the order numbers' step (they are only compared), a start value below every order, and the inflater's output limit (the length check after it holds either way). Seven more were found by the first full run and killed by tests added for them (`verify` on a closed bundle with no advisories; the same package listed twice with another forgotten; a package number and an advisory number one past the end; a warning count of exactly 2^40).
  - **Not covered:** the real OSV bundle (the sandbox cannot reach it: its size, how well it compresses, and the load time on a laptop are measured on the synthetic one; the first nightly build on a runner with network gives the real figures: `python3 scripts/profile/sca_bundle.py compare cve-bundle.json` prints the load and match seconds, the peak memory and the file size for both kinds on it, and fails if the matches differ; `gen N out.json` makes the synthetic bundle); making the indexed bundle the default (one line in `sca_feeds.run_update` plus README and CHANGELOG, after the real numbers); `--bundle-format` and the `.lzx` format in README, CHANGELOG and `docs/` (the 0.1.8 agent's files until the tag); Go and crates.io advisories (above); an npm twin (the SCA is Python only); memory-mapping (a plain seek and read is portable and a file that shrinks under it fails a read, not the process).
  - **The comparison script:** `scripts/profile/sca_bundle.py` (`compare`, `gen`, `inventory`, `build`, `measure`; 12 tests in `test_profile_scripts.py`): each side runs in a process of its own so the peak memory is the format's, the inventory is half names the bundle has and half it has not, and the answers are hashed (in order, ranges with sorted keys) and must be equal.
- **P-10: where the time goes, and a nightly check that it stays there** (`scripts/profile/`; tests `tests/architecture/test_profile_scripts.py`, 51). Standard library plus the checkout and the native engine; every script imports `lazaret` from the tree it sits in. README in the directory.
  - **`perf_check.py` + `packages.json`**: the eight packages of the profile (litellm, botocore, requests, express, playwright-core, typescript, next, eslint) as the exact archives a registry scan reads, pinned by sha256 (`fetch` refuses any other bytes; `pin npm:next@16.4.0` adds or moves one). `run --runs 3` times only the engine: archives are fetched and hashed first and read into memory, so the network is never in the numbers; each run scans all the archives of a package with `repo._scan_artifact`, and the figure is the seconds inside the native engine summed over its threads, the median of three, after one unmeasured scan of the smallest archive for the engine's set-up. Every run reads the same files: P-14, merged, bounds the use-time step by characters, so there is no box to raise.
  - **Budgets are empty on purpose**: a package without one is reported and never failed. `calibrate r1.json r2.json r3.json --write` proposes each as the median of the medians plus 25% (at least 0.25 s) and records the number of files scanned, so a later run that reads a different number says "work changed" instead of failing on someone else's change. Do it after a week of nightly runs (download the `perf-report` artifacts).
  - **The profile harness** (`registry_scan.py`, `engine_replay.py`, `release_overlap.py`, `thread_scaling.py`, `use_time_coverage.py`, `guard_connections.py`): the scripts behind the numbers of the profile doc, each with `--help`; a cold run asks the registries and can keep their answers in `--cache DIR`, a warm run replays them.
  - **`.github/workflows/perf.yml`** (written and checked with `zizmor`; nightly and `workflow_dispatch`, the rules of `ci.yml`, no secrets). It was first a download for John, since `.github/` is a protected path for this tooling; John put it in the project folder and said to write it into `.github/workflows/`, and it is in the branch from the commit that adds `fuzz.yml` too (copied byte for byte). `WorkflowTests` in the test file run now (they skipped while the file was absent). A schedule runs only from the default branch, so the first real run is after the merge.
  - Not measured here: the numbers need the real network for `fetch`; the first calibrated budgets come from nightly runs on GitHub's runners, not from this sandbox.
- **X-1: fuzzers for the readers of untrusted input** (`scripts/fuzz/`: `fuzz.py`, `fuzz_targets.py`, `fuzz_mutate.py`, `fuzz_common.py`, README; tests `tests/architecture/test_fuzz_scripts.py`, 64; the findings in `docs/0.1.9-findings.md`). Standard library only; no new dependency.
  - **Seventeen targets**: `archive-tgz`, `-tbz2`, `-txz`, `-zip` (`repo.iter_archive`: every member a real size, a safe relative path, within the limits; a budget or limit stops the reading; the same bytes give the same members), `xml` and `xml-minidom` (`safexml`: only the documented errors, a bounded number of elements, no amplification), and eleven lockfile and manifest readers through `sca.scan_all` (`package-lock.json`, `yarn.lock`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `bun.lock`, `poetry.lock`, `uv.lock`, `pylock.toml`, `Pipfile.lock`, `requirements.txt`, `pyproject.toml`, `setup.py`: no exception, no warning on stderr, an inventory no larger than the file).
  - **Design**: seeded (`--seed`; a seed is the same inputs and the same findings), the seed corpus first and then byte and structure mutations with a dictionary per target; a time-guided corpus (an input 4 times slower than the average is kept, so a quadratic reader is found); findings de-duplicated by kind and signature, shrunk by deleting chunks, written as a `.bin` and a `.txt` (traceback, seed, run number, Python); kinds are an exception, a warning (not a deprecation), a broken promise and a slow input; `--hard-limit` ends a hung input with every thread's stack (`faulthandler`) and leaves it in `--pending`; `--replay FILE TARGET`; exit 1 for a finding that is not in `fuzz_targets.KNOWN`.
  - **Tested** (64 tests, 8 s, run by the suite): the mutator (same seed, same bytes; nothing outgrows its limit; each change does what it says; an empty input), the driver with fake targets (a clean target runs clean; each kind is a finding; de-duplication, shrinking, exit status, replay), the real targets (seeds run clean; a short run of fixed seed finds nothing new; each `KNOWN` finding still reproduces, so the entry has to go with the fix; and every promise a target checks is shown live by a reader that breaks it), the README names every target and finding, and `fuzz.yml` follows `ci.yml`'s rules when the file is there.
  - **Seven findings** (details, repros and fixes in `docs/0.1.9-findings.md`, mirrored in the project doc `audits/lazaret-fuzz-findings-2026-10-03.md`). Fixed here, in `scanner/sca.py` (on the allow-list; tests `tests/scanner/test_sca_fuzz_findings.py`, 11): **F-3** a NUL byte in a requirements `-r` path raised `ValueError` out of `scan_all`; **F-4** a TOML file nested too deeply raised `RecursionError` out of `tomllib` past every caller that catches `ValueError` (the inventory, the guard's `uv.lock` reader, the package-manager settings); **F-5** a `setup.py` with an invalid escape printed a `SyntaxWarning`. For the 0.1.8 agent (`repo.py`, `safexml`; reported, not edited): **F-1** an encoding declaration expat cannot use (`encoding="T7"`, `"UTF32"`) raises `LookupError` / `ValueError`, as the standard library does, instead of `ParseError`; **F-2** a zip with entries that overlap makes `zipfile` warn on stderr (3.12.3+) and records no anomaly; **F-6** a zip entry with no name raises `IndexError` out of `_iter_zip` on Python 3.10 (`ZipInfo.is_dir()` reads the name's last character) and is skipped without a word on 3.11+; **F-7** an LZMA zip entry that declares a 4 GiB dictionary raises `MemoryError` where memory is capped (an address-space limit, a small VM).
  - **Runs**: Python 3.13 (this sandbox) 1,030,717 inputs over 16 targets (seeds 11 to 13, 20 s each; `xml-minidom` in the short runs), and Python 3.10 (the device) 1,268,422 inputs over all 17 (seeds 21 and 22, 60 and 80 s, 4 processes at once): only the known findings besides F-6 and F-7, which the 3.10 runs found within a minute each. Each Python shows what the other cannot (F-2 is `zipfile` 3.12.3+, F-4 is `tomllib` 3.11+, F-6 is 3.10's `ZipInfo`, F-7 depends on the memory limit): the weekly job runs both ends.
  - **`.github/workflows/fuzz.yml`** (placed as `perf.yml` was): weekly (Sunday 04:23 UTC) and on demand, Python 3.10 and 3.13, a seed from the run, `--hard-limit 120`, the findings as an artifact (90 days), actions pinned to SHAs, `contents: read`, no secrets, no caches; `zizmor` clean. A finding that is not in `KNOWN` fails the job. Its `WorkflowTests` run now.
  - **Not done**: no fuzzer yet for `js_parse` / `py_parse` / the taint passes (the Rust harness takes a `Lang`, so a language is one `langs()` entry), for `pmsettings`' file readers (`.npmrc`, `pip.conf`, `bunfig.toml`, `.yarnrc.yml`: the next targets, small), for the JS twin of `sca`, or for the guard's JSON-document readers.

- **P-2a, the memo: ask the engine once for bytes that appear in many archives of a release** (`registry/contentcache.py`, `6f40d08`; tests `tests/registry/test_contentcache.py`, 72; design `specs/lazaret-content-cache-design-2026-10-03.md`). In memory, for one run, no stored state, so no trust question. **Nothing calls it yet**: the call sites (`scan_pending`, `_import_time_risks`, `_cross_file_code`) are the 0.1.8 agent's, and with the memo written first each is a few-line edit.
  - **What is kept is the engine's raw answer for one input, never a verdict.** The key is the kind of call, the path, the SHA-256 of the text, the language, the flags, the rule pack and the engine; the verdict is decided afterwards from the issues of the whole artifact, so a hit changes the time and nothing else. `NULL` is the memo that is always off, with the same interface, so the gate is "identical issues with the memo on and off".
  - **Single flight**: two threads that want one key do the work once; a call that raises raises for every thread that waited for it and is not stored. **Nothing unfinished is stored**: `compute` returns `Uncacheable(answer)` when it ran out of time or budget (the caller gets it, the memo keeps nothing, a waiter works it out for itself, since its budget may differ). **A hit is a copy**, in and out. **Bounded by bytes**: least recently used first, and an answer over 1/8 of the bound is not kept. **No hold and wait**: a batch computes the keys it claimed before it waits for any key another thread claimed, so two batches that overlap cannot wait for each other. A waiter's patience (`wait`) is its own.
  - **Mutation-checked** (`dfdf298`): 211 mutants, 194 killed at first. The survivors were read: new tests killed every one that changed behaviour, and two were equivalent (a redundant `0 < max_bytes and`, a second `move_to_end`), so that code was removed.
  - **Not covered**: real savings. The profile's estimate (litellm 76 s to about 26 s) needs the call sites; the first measurement is the `perf_check.py` run after the 0.1.8 agent has wired the memo in.

- **The registry module interface: `registry/ecosystems/base.py` and a conformance mixin** (`b89bf76`; `archive_root` in `eacdd12`; fixes in `9e9c47a`; tests `tests/registry/test_ecosystems_base.py`, 104; the mixin is `tests/registry/ecosystem_contract.py`; doc `specs/lazaret-registry-module-interface-2026-10-03.md`). X-2, right after P-2a, as John asked. Python only (registry modules have no npm twin). `repo.py` is unchanged: it can adopt the interface after the tag, one ecosystem at a time.
  - **`Ecosystem`**, one class per registry: names and versions (`check_name`, `check_version`, `identity`, `parse_spec`, `segment`), `resolve(name, version, fetch)` to a `Resolution` (the artifacts, their digests, `info`), `verify`, `member_path`, `links_extracted`, `run_targets(kind, manifests, members)` and `declared` (what runs at install and at start; what a package declares), and `archive_root(resolved, artifact)` (the directory every member sits under, with its trailing slash, or None; `repo.py` would pass it as `iter_archive(root=...)`).
  - **`Fetch`, the one seam to the network**: https only, declared hosts only, redirects re-checked against the hosts, a per-host rate, byte limits, and `bytes` / `text` / `json` / `json_lines(select=)` (a large index read line by line, keeping only what the module selects). A module under test gets a fake `Fetch` and never touches the network.
  - **`base.show(value, limit)`** quotes hostile text for a message and is bounded after escaping, not before (F-8). **`HOSTILE_NAMES` / `HOSTILE_VERSIONS`** are the shared lists of bad input (controls, bidi, NUL, lone surrogates, percent-escapes, very long, long escapes) that the mixin runs through every module.
  - **The mixin is the contract**: a new ecosystem inherits it and sets a few attributes (`GOOD_NAMES`, `GOOD_VERSIONS`, `GOOD_SPEC`, `MEMBER_PATHS`, `GOOD_ARCHIVE_MEMBERS`, `DIGEST_COVERS_EVERY_BYTE` and `changed_downloads` for a digest of contents, ...). It checks that every message is short and printable (`BODY_LIMIT` 600 characters), that hostile names and versions raise only `SpecError`, that nothing is fetched for a name that fails, that `verify` refuses every changed download, and that `resolve` asks only its declared hosts.
  - **Mutation-checked** (`base.py`): 193 mutants, 175 killed at first; the 18 survivors were mostly the sizes in the hostile lists (not behaviour) and the edges of the URL and line limits and of `select`, and tests for those edges were added in `9e9c47a`. The lines added later (the budget, `Declared`): 66 of 66 killed. A full re-run on the final file: 263 mutants, 243 killed; the 20 survivors were read: 13 are the sizes of the hostile samples (`"x" * 5000` against `5001`), three are equivalent (`while True` in the drive-root loop, which breaks anyway; `if rest else ""`, where `normpath("")` is already `"."`; `pos < end` against `pos <= end` in the line reader), and four were in `show`'s shrink step, where a `min()` could never be the smaller side: it was removed, and `show`'s cut is now tested to be as long as the limit allows (exact outputs for three hostile inputs; the 15 mutants of that step were all killed on the re-run).

- **R-1, crates.io** (`registry/ecosystems/crates.py`, `f5246a2`; tests `tests/registry/test_crates.py`, 106; recorded responses in `tests/registry/recorded/crates/`: `fnv` whole, `paste` and `Inflector` index lines and manifests). Resolves a crate through the sparse index, downloads the `.crate` from `static.crates.io`, checks its SHA-256 against the index's `cksum`, and says which files run at build.
  - Only the two CDN hosts are declared and no rate (the 1 request per second of crates.io's policy is for its API, which is not used). The index is read with `json_lines`, keeping per version only what is needed (a large crate's `features` are most of its file). A spec uses the spelling the crate is registered under: the index file for `foo_bar` is not the one for `foo-bar`, and the download URL needs the index's own spelling (`Inflector/Inflector-0.11.4.crate`; the lowercase form is refused).
  - **What runs**: `build.rs` (or the path `package.build` names, unless it is `false`) and a proc-macro crate's library root are `install_scripts`; the library root and the binaries are `entries`; `startup` is empty (nothing runs when a crate is merely loaded). Dev-dependencies are not declared.
  - **Archive**: cargo refuses a crate with any member that is not a file or a directory, or that sits outside `<name>-<version>/`, and skips `.cargo-ok`. So `links_extracted` is False, `.cargo-ok` is not a member, and `archive_root` is `name-version/`.
  - **Latest** is the highest version that is not yanked, a release before a pre-release (what `cargo add` picks); a version asked for by name is resolved even if yanked and `info["yanked"]` says so.
  - **Checked**: the contract mixin; the recorded `fnv` crate hashes to the index's `cksum`; seven boundary tests (each limit exactly: name, version, versions per index, dependencies per line, `rust-version` and `links`, manifest paths, target tables). **Mutation** (`crates.py`): 267 mutants, 252 killed at first; the 15 survivors were read, boundary tests were added, and the targeted re-runs of the lines concerned (32 mutants) were all killed.
  - **`declared`** (`03a177a`) gives the registry names of the normal and build dependencies, each one's version requirement as written (a string, or the table's `version`; the first table that names it; at most 512 characters) and the renames (`foo = { package = "bar" }` is `{"bar": "foo"}`), which the unused-dependency check needs (the 0.1.8 agent's #156): the name the code uses is the alias.
  - **Not covered**: the guards are R-3 (done: see the R-3 block below); `run_targets` reads `Cargo.toml`, not `Cargo.lock`. (`Cargo.lock` into SCA, with RustSec through OSV's `crates.io` ecosystem, is done: see the next blocks.)

- **G-1, the Go module system** (`registry/ecosystems/golang.py`, `b4dc9b3`, fixes `9e9c47a`; tests `tests/registry/test_golang.py`, 147; recorded `tests/registry/recorded/go/`; the oracle `scripts/gooracle/`, tests `tests/architecture/test_gooracle_script.py`, 22). Resolves a module through the proxy (`@latest` or `@v/<version>.info`), reads the checksum database's record for it, downloads the module zip, checks it, and says what is declared and which files run.
  - **Rules read from Go's own code** (`golang.org/x/mod`): module paths (`CheckPath`, the `!` case escape, `SplitPathVersion` with `gopkg.in`, `CheckPathMajor`), versions (`CanonicalVersion`, pseudo-versions), `go.mod` (`modfile.ParseLax`, a dependency's reading: unknown directives ignored, `module ""` accepted, `// indirect` counted), module-zip members (`CheckZip`; 500 MiB of content, 16 MiB for `go.mod`, a `go.mod` below the root is not part of the module), and `h1:` (base64 of the SHA-256 of the sorted lines `"%x  %s\n"`, one per member).
  - **The h1 hash is the zip's content, not its bytes**: Go accepts a zip with bytes after the end record, so `DIGEST_COVERS_EVERY_BYTE` is False for Go and the mixin tests changed members instead (`changed_downloads`). Two members of one name, or starting at one place, are refused with `DigestError` (F-9).
  - **Checksum database**: `parse_lookup` reads `/lookup/<module>@<version>` (the record number, the `<module> <version> h1:` line and the `/go.mod` line). **The signed tree head and the tile proofs are not checked**: the lookup is read over TLS from `sum.golang.org`, and `info["sumdb"]` says `"tls"` so a report can say so. A pure-Python Ed25519 check and tile proof (the real key is in `x/mod`'s `modfetch/key.go`) is the next slice, if John wants it. (Done on tiny_https instead: NET-1, part 3, below.)
  - **What runs**: nothing at download, and `go build` runs no generator. `run_targets` marks by name: `main.go` and `cmd/*.go` are `entries`, the C, assembly and object files the Go tool hands to other tools are `install_scripts`. Which `.go` file declares `init` needs the text, which the interface does not pass (see below); G-2 and G-3 read it.
  - **The oracle** (`scripts/gooracle/`): `main.go` is a batch program over `golang.org/x/mod` v0.22.0 (the copy in Go 1.24.7's `cmd/vendor`), one JSON line in and one out: `checkpath`, `escapepath`, `split`, `semver`, `canonver`, `checkmajor`, `parsemodlax`, `checkzip`, `hashzip`, `hashfile`, `vcszip` (a module zip made from a git tag, as the proxy makes one), and `sumdbserve` (the checksum database's own server code over records we choose). `gooracle.py` has `build`, `diff` and `golden`. **`diff` at scale 60 (44,000 paths, 54,000 versions, 4,700 zip members, 15,000 `go.mod` files of which Go accepts 5,600, 2,000 zips) and at scale 20: no difference.** `golden` writes `recorded/go/golden.json` (Go's answers, so the module's tests need no Go) and two real module zips whose `h1:` equals the value in published `go.sum` files (`go-difflib` v1.0.0, `errors` v0.9.1; `go-spew` v1.1.1 was checked the same way and not kept). The tests never run Go; run `diff` when `golang.py` changes or when a Go release changes `x/mod`.
  - **Mutation** (`golang.py`): 702 mutants, 635 killed at first (67 survivors). Four lines were dead code (a leading slash or dash in the first element, which the element check refuses before it; a short version with a pre-release or build part, which the pattern does not match) and were removed; the others were limits not at their edge, messages not read to the end, and Go's short and odd paths, which 14 new tests cover with Go's own answers (asked of the oracle). The full re-run on the final file: 695 mutants, 667 killed, 28 survivors, all read. Twenty-four are equivalent (`return False` against `None` where the caller tests truth; `split(x, 1)` against `split(x, 2)` where only the first or last piece is used; `while True`; a read's chunk size; the tilde-and-digits line's `<` against `<=` and its `isascii` tests, which the element check has already made true; `i <= 1`, `len(major) <= 2` and `i > 0` in the major-version split, which guard indexes Python's negative indexing would not fault on; a type test before an `elif` chain that is false for `None` anyway; `str(name)` of a string; a backslash at the very end of a quoted string, which is unterminated either way). Three were real and are now killed by tests that carry Go's answers: `isdigit` is wider than Go's `'0' <= c <= '9'` (an Arabic-Indic digit after `x.y/v` or `gopkg.in/x.v`), and `require ( v1.0.0` is a require of the path `(` for Go, not a block. One (an encrypted zip entry's name decoded as UTF-8 instead of CP437) matters only for an encrypted directory entry, since an encrypted file fails closed before the hash is used; it is left. The oracle's `diff` was run again after the dead code came out (scale 6 and 60): no difference. The targeted re-run of the lines changed (106 mutants): 99 killed; the 7 survivors are six of the equivalent kinds above and an `args[:2]` for `args[:1]` in the block-open test, whose condition (`args[:1] == ["("] and len(args) == 1`) was then simplified to `args == ["("]`.
  - **Not covered**: **the proxy's answers are protocol-shaped, not recorded** (the sandbox cannot reach `proxy.golang.org` or `sum.golang.org`): `.info`, `@latest`, `.mod` and the lookup body are written from `go help goproxy` and Go's code (the lookup bodies come from Go's own server code, signed with a key made from a fixed seed). The first run on a machine with network should record them into `recorded/go/` (the two `curl` lines are in its README). Retracted versions are not handled. The interface passes `run_targets` only manifests and names; an optional `wants_text(member)` hook is the proposal (to be added to the interface doc). The guards (G-3) are done (see the G-3 block below), and so is `go.sum` in SCA (see the SCA block).

- **Eight fuzz targets for the registry modules** (`7c0ca50`; `scripts/fuzz/fuzz_targets.py`, README rows; `RegistryModulePromisesAreLive` in `test_fuzz_scripts.py`, now 77 tests): `crates-index`, `crates-manifest`, `go-zip`, `go-mod`, `go-sumdb`, `go-resolve`, `ecosystem-names`, `ecosystem-member-path`; 27 targets in all. Each checks the promise its module makes (only the documented error types; short printable messages; a bounded number of requests, only to declared hosts; versions that are versions and the one asked for; the same input twice gives the same answer) and has a reader that breaks it, to show the check can fail.
  - **Two real findings, both fixed here** (details in `docs/0.1.9-findings.md`): **F-8** an error message that quoted a hostile name or version could be over 1,200 characters, since `base.show` cut the text to 120 characters and then escaped it (a lone surrogate is 6 characters, a code point past the BMP 10); `show` now cuts after escaping. **F-9** a Go module zip with two members starting at one place made CPython (3.12.3 and later) print a `UserWarning` while the hash was read; the module now refuses it with `DigestError` first. F-9 is the same shape as F-2 (`repo._iter_zip`, the 0.1.8 agent's), which is still open.
  - **Mistakes in my own checks that the runs showed** (so the checks themselves are tested): bound methods compared with `is` (a new object each time), a name stripped before the rules saw it, `module ""` is valid for Go's lax parser, a determinism check that compared the wrong parts.

- **Taking in the 0.1.8 agent's answers** (`03a177a`; `specs/lazaret-registry-module-interface-answers-2026-10-03.md`, `specs/lazaret-content-cache-answers-2026-10-03.md`; the account is `specs/lazaret-registry-modules-as-built-2026-10-03.md`). New files only, as before.
  - **The cross-file key was wrong and is fixed.** `contentcache.cross_file_key(files, flags, pack, engine)` sorted the files and ignored their languages, which treated the question as a set; the engine's answer is not a function of the set. It now takes `[(path, language, content)]` in the order given and keeps the order; a pair, a four-tuple or a bare string for a file is a `ValueError`. The docstring says what a caller must return as `Uncacheable` (an unanswered call, a `NativeError`, a cross-file answer with a package marked `failed`) and that `engine.cross_file_issues` drops that mark today, so it is the 0.1.8 agent's to pass up. Tests 69 to 72.
  - **`Declared(name, dependencies, specs, aliases)`**: each dependency's spec as written (or None) and the renames, for SC-UNUSED-DEPENDENCY. The constructor raises `ValueError` for a spec or alias of a name that is not a dependency. Filled by crates.io (see above) and by Go (the first `require` line's version for each module; no aliases). Three new fuzz checks (`crates-manifest-specs`, `crates-manifest-aliases`, `go-mod-declared-specs`), each with a reader that breaks it.
  - **`DownloadBudget(total)`** for P-9: `reserve(n)` is one step under a lock (two threads cannot both take the last share) and `give_back(n)`; `Fetch.bytes(url, max_bytes, accept, budget=None)` reserves `max_bytes` before any request (a spent budget is a `FetchError` and no request, and no turn of a rate-limited host) and gives back what the body did not use, or everything when the request failed. The rate limiter's state is under a lock (`Fetch._lock`), tested with 8 threads; the contract calls `resolve` from four threads and requires one answer. Tested with 16 threads wanting one share of a budget that holds 10 of them, and 8 threads reserving and giving back 500 times.
  - **The dedupe key is `repo.py`'s own SHA-256 of the downloaded bytes**, never the registry's digest: Go's `h1:` is a hash of the zip's members (Go accepts a zip with bytes after the end record, or with its members in another order), so `verify` says whether the download is the module and is not a key. The mixin has `DIGEST_COVERS_EVERY_BYTE` (False for Go).
  - **Mutation after the changes**: new lines of `base.py` 66 of 66, `contentcache.py` 12 of 12, `crates.py` 39 of 39 killed; the full re-runs of `base.py` and `golang.py` are in the blocks above.

- **Go and Rust in SCA, and one `go.mod` reader** (`7e0272d`; `scanner/gomod.py` (new), `scanner/sca.py`, `scanner/sca_feeds.py`, `scanner/sca_index.py`; tests `tests/scanner/test_gomod.py` 23, `tests/scanner/test_sca_go_crates.py` 106, and the feeds and index tests, now 73 and 77). The lockfile halves of G-2 and R-2, and the OSV exports for both ecosystems in the bundle. Python only (SCA has no npm twin).
  - **Inventory** (ecosystems `go` and `crates`). `go.mod`: every `require`, indirect ones too, with the `replace` lines applied (a replacement by a version of the module is that version; by another module, that module, and the module required stays in with no version, so its advisories report unknown; by a directory it is the project's own code and is not inventoried). `go.sum`: read only when the `go.mod` has no `go` directive or one before 1.17 (a later `go.mod` lists what the build needs), the highest version of each module that has a zip hash. `vendor/modules.txt`. `Cargo.lock`: a registry crate at the locked version, a crate from git or any other source with no version, a crate with no source is a path dependency or workspace member and is the project's own. The root `Cargo.toml`: dependencies, dev- and build-dependencies, target tables and `[workspace.dependencies]`; `=1.2.3` is a version, any other requirement a range, kept with no version; `package = "real"` names the crate.
  - **Names** are the registries' own identities: a Go module path is kept as written (it is case-sensitive, and `.`, `-` and `_` are not alike in it); a crate folds case and `_` to `-` (crates.io holds one of `a-b` and `a_b`). A path whose first element has no dot (`stdlib`, `toolchain`) is not a module Go can fetch, and the standard library is not inventoried (a `go.mod` names a minimum Go version, not the toolchain).
  - **Advisories**: the bundle builder reads OSV's `Go` and `crates.io` exports (ids `GO-` and `RUSTSEC-`, ordered after `MAL-`). Versions are compared as SemVer only (Go's `v` prefix and pseudo-versions included). A Go advisory about the standard library or the toolchain is skipped, and so is a RustSec advisory that is not a flaw in a version (`unmaintained`, `notice`): there is nothing to match. `sources` in the bundle (`osv:go`, `osv:crates`) gate a fail-closed verdict **only when the inventory has Go or crates entries**: an older bundle that lacks them cannot say "no advisories" for a Go or Rust project.
  - **The shared reader**: `scanner/gomod.py` is `modfile`'s lexer and rules (canonical versions, the replace and exclude rules, `go` directive comparison), used by the Go module auditor (`registry/ecosystems/golang.py`, now a client of it) and by the inventory. The scanner does not import the registry package, so the reader lives in `scanner/`. The oracle (`scripts/gooracle`) has a new op `parsemod` (x/mod v0.22.0) and `diff` compares the project's reading of `go.mod` with Go's: no mismatch at scale 5 and 6.
  - **Five fuzz targets** (`sca-go-mod`, `sca-go-sum`, `sca-vendor-modules-txt`, `sca-cargo-lock`, `sca-cargo-toml`; 32 in all): `scan_all` over one file of the kind raises nothing and prints nothing, the inventory is no larger than the file, and a Go module is a path Go could fetch at a SemVer version with a `v`, or at none.
  - **Mutation**: `gomod.py` 240 mutants, the survivors read (one equivalent); the Go and crates lines of `sca.py` 283 mutants, 259 killed. The 24 survivors were read: the cap constants at one past their limit (`MAX_GO_SUM_LINES`, `MAX_GO_MODULES`), the 80-character truncations of messages, existing-file checks that a missing file makes true anyway, and two edges worth a test that were left (the field count of a `go.sum` line; the size cap of `Cargo.toml`).
  - **Not covered**: `go.work` (`use` directories), a `go.mod` below the root, the member manifests of a Cargo workspace (the lock covers them), RustSec's `unmaintained` and `notice` as low-severity findings (backlog), the standard library through the toolchain (through the `toolchain` and `go` lines: backlog), (the guards for `go` and `cargo`, G-3 and R-3, are done: see their blocks below).

- **G-2, the Go parser** (`rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/goparse/`: `mod.rs`, `scan.rs`, `parser.rs`, `tree.rs`, `out.rs`, `tests.rs`; the example `examples/goparse_dump.rs`; `scripts/goparse/`: `astdump.go`, `diff.py`, `mutate.py`, `README.md`; tests `tests/architecture/test_goparse_scripts.py`, 36). New files and one additive `pub mod goparse;` in `lib.rs`; **nothing calls it yet** (the detectors and the registration in `api.rs` come after the tag).
  - **A port of `go/parser` (Go 1.24, `SkipObjectResolution`) over its own scanner**, on code points. It accepts exactly the files `go/parser` accepts and builds the same tree: an arena of 28-byte nodes (kind, operator, flags, start, end, four slots), with `go/ast`'s kind names and spans; no `Bad*` node, since it stops at the first error; comments are skipped (a detector reads them from `lex::structure`, and the cgo preamble is the comment before `import "C"`, found by position). Traversal is a loop, `ast.Inspect`'s order (`Tree::preorder`); chains (`a.b.c`, `x + x`, `if … else if …`, `f()()`) are loops too and cost no depth.
  - **Held to Go's own parser** (`scripts/goparse/README.md`): the oracle `astdump.go` prints every node's kind and span for each file; `diff.py` compares. On the source and tests of Go 1.24.7 (10,368 files) 10,245 trees are the same, 122 files are refused by both, and one is refused for depth; not one node differs; 1.7 s for the corpus. On about 430,000 mutants of those files (`mutate.py`, nine seeds) the two agree on every file that does not nest past the limit. The mutants found three differences, all fixed and kept as cases in `tests.rs`: a NUL or a byte order mark inside a comment or a string, a malformed `//line` directive (only when it starts a line), and a carriage return in a raw string (`go/ast` counts it out of the literal's end).
  - **The limit** (the one deliberate difference): `MAX_DEPTH` 256 (statements, expressions, types and composite literals nested), "exceeded max nesting depth"; `go/parser` stops at 100,000. The engine reads untrusted files on a stack of 1 MiB to 8 MiB, and `tests.rs` pins the deepest nesting read for each of the 21 constructs that nest and checks that the deepest of each fits a 1 MiB stack. One file of the Go distribution (`test/fixedbugs/issue29312.go`) nests deeper and is refused.
  - **Tests** (`cargo test -p lazaret-engine goparse`, 14; release and debug): a small file's tree, 34 accepted and 39 refused files (each checked against `go/parser`), where and why errors are reported, the nesting table, the stack, chains, spans, code-point counting, flags, tree invariants on every prefix and one-code-point edit of the accepted files and on 20,000 seeded soups of tokens and arbitrary code points, and linear time. Changing `MAX_DEPTH` by one fails the table. **Scripts**: `diff.py` 70 of 72 and `mutate.py` 110 of 112 mutants killed (the two left in each are the `__main__` guard).
  - **Not done**: no `fuzz_goparse` example in the style of `fuzz_lex` (the differential run with `mutate.py` is the fuzzer, and needs Go); the arena keeps a few nodes the parser replaced (an `if` condition's `ExprStmt`), unreachable from the root; the registration of the detectors (the Rust parser, R-2, and the hooks for both languages are done: see below).

- **G-3, `lazaret guard go`** (`cb05cea`; `registry/guard.py` (the Go section), `registry/goproxy.py` (new), `registry/ecosystems/golang.py`, `_cli.py`; tests `tests/registry/test_guard_go.py` 122, `tests/registry/test_goproxy.py` 30, the fake go in `tests/registry/_go_support.py`). `lazaret guard go get | install | build | run | test | vet | list | mod download | mod tidy …`: the go command runs against a Go module proxy of the guard's on this machine, which relays the proxies `GOPROXY` lists and scans each module zip before the command gets it. Python only.
  - **A relay, not a wrapper around go's output.** `GOPROXY` is set, for the one command, to a local server (`LocalGoProxy`, 127.0.0.1, a port the OS gives). It answers the proxy protocol (`/<module>/@v/list`, `@latest`, `<version>.info`, `.mod`, `.zip`, and the checksum database's paths, which are relayed as they are) from the proxies `GOPROXY` named, **in the order and by the rules of the go command**: a comma goes on to the next proxy on a 404 or 410 only, a bar on any error. A module's zip is fetched, scanned and handed over only when it is not blocked (a 403 with the reason otherwise); **go then checks the bytes against `go.sum` or the checksum database itself**, so what go accepts is what was scanned. Only the hosts of the listed proxies are fetched from; the credentials of a private proxy (userinfo in its URL, `.netrc`) are the relay's, and the tool never sees them.
  - **`--min-age`** holds versions back at the proxy: a module's list leaves out those younger than the age (the newest 24, `GO_PROBES`, are looked up), and an `.info` or a zip of one is refused, so `go get` falls back to the newest version old enough. The time is the one the proxy gives (the commit or tag time as its author recorded it).
