  - **What it does not guess about**: `direct` in the list is not honoured (the guard does not fetch from version control; it says so, and a module the proxy lacks is not fetched); modules `GOPRIVATE` / `GONOPROXY` name go fetches itself, and the guard lists those it finds in the module cache afterwards as "fetched straight from its repository: not checked"; anything else that appears in the module cache without having come through the proxy is listed as unchecked, with a hint (`GOFLAGS`, a vendor folder). `GO111MODULE=off`, a `GOPROXY` of `off` or `direct` alone, and a proxy that is not http or https stop with a message that says why. A toolchain download (`golang.org/toolchain`) is checked by go against the checksum database, is not held back by `--min-age`, and the report says it was not scanned.
  - **Put back**: `go.mod`, `go.sum`, `go.work` and `go.work.sum` (and the `-modfile` pair) are put back when anything was blocked, as for npm and pip. `--plan` runs only the fetching part of the command (`go get` or `go mod download`; for `install` and `run` of `pkg@version`, `go get` in an empty module of its own) against a scratch module cache, so nothing is written into the project and nothing is built or run. An interrupt tears the proxy, its threads and the spool down (`TeardownTests`, `InterruptTests`).
  - **Tests**: the relay's answers for every request kind and every error, GOPROXY lists (comma, bar, `direct`, `off`, globs for `GOPRIVATE`), the age rules with a lone young version and with only young versions, the zip and `.info` cases, the HTTP server whatever the relay does (HEAD, a missing zip, an error, an exception inside it), the flows with a fake go that does what the test says (a command that fetches, one that fails, one blocked, `--plan`, the cache), and `RealGoTests` against the real go command when `go` is on the path. **Mutation** (`guard.py`, Go section): 427 mutants in the Go section: 343 killed at first, 84 survived; tests were added for the real ones, and the survivors' lines were run again twice (156 mutants, then 69), leaving 16, each read: the zip reply's status field (a file or a stream is sent as 200 whatever it says), a scratch folder made when it is not needed and removed either way, `finish` forcing `EXIT_BLOCKED` when anything is blocked before the caller's own choice, the truthiness of a one-item slice (`args[1:2]`), a 404-or-410 test that the relay's own loop has already settled, the parallel pre-fetch of `.info` answers (it only saves time), a thread's daemon flag, and the size of a copy buffer. `goproxy.py`: 208 mutants, 206 killed; the two left are equivalent (a glob that ends right after a backslash or a range dash is malformed either way).
  - **Not covered**: the proxy's answers in the tests are protocol-shaped, written from `go help goproxy` and Go's code (the sandbox cannot reach `proxy.golang.org`); the first run on a machine with network should check one real `go get` through it. A module go fetches itself (`GOPRIVATE`) is not scanned. `go.work` `use` directories are not looked at. The content scan of a module's `.go` files and the Go hooks wait for S-4.

- **R-3, `lazaret guard cargo`** (`cb05cea`; `registry/cargosrc.py` (new), `registry/guard.py` (the Cargo section), `_cli.py`; tests `tests/registry/test_guard_cargo.py` 125, `tests/registry/test_cargosrc.py` 41, the fake cargo in `tests/registry/_cargo_support.py`). `lazaret guard cargo add | update | generate-lockfile | install | fetch | build | check | clippy | doc | test | bench | run | fix | vendor | package …` (with a `+toolchain` first, as rustup takes it). Python only.
  - **Resolve first, then check, then build.** cargo has no local-server hook for crates the way pip, npm and go do (a registry's index and its `dl` URL are in configuration, and the replaced source is the user's), so the guard makes the resolution itself: `cargo update --workspace` (no network when `--offline`), reads `Cargo.lock`, **fetches every crates.io crate the lock names that cargo has not already unpacked** from where cargo would (the registry's `config.json` `dl`; cargo's own cache when it holds the `.crate`), checks its **SHA-256 against the lock's `checksum`** (the index's `cksum` when the lock has none), scans it, and only then lets cargo fetch and build. `--locked` / `--frozen` skip the resolution and check the lock as it is. For `add`, `update` and `generate-lockfile` the command is the resolution and what it added to `Cargo.lock` is what is checked.
  - **`--min-age`** reads the release time from the index's `pubtime` (when the registry gives it) and else from crates.io's API (one request a second, the API's rule); a crate with a time from neither has no age, and `--min-age` does not hold it back.
  - **Where crates come from**: `[source.*]` replacement in the configuration files (`cargosrc.sources`) is applied, so a mirror's sparse index is the one read, and its `dl` is allowed for the fetch and no other host is. **A crate that cannot be checked is blocked** (the guard fails closed: HTTP error, no checksum, a hash that is not the lock's), except those it was never asked to read, which are noted in the report as not checked: a crate from git, from a git-index registry or from a source it does not know, crates.io replaced by a git index, and a registry that answers 401 or 403 (credentials the guard does not read). **The crates cargo unpacks that the guard did not check are listed after the build** (`unchecked`), with a hint, so the user can see what was not covered. A path dependency is the project's own code.
  - **`cargo install <crate>…`** resolves in a scratch project that needs only that crate (what `cargo install` resolves; `--locked` takes the `Cargo.lock` published with the crate), checks every crates.io crate in that lock, and then installs. Nothing is written into the user's project.
  - **Put back**: `Cargo.toml` of every workspace member and `Cargo.lock` are restored when anything was blocked, and when `--plan` was asked. Every name and version that reaches a URL or a path is checked first (`cargosrc.crate_ok`), so a hostile lockfile can name nothing but a crate to fetch.
  - **Tests**: sources and registry replacement, lock reading, the index line reader (`pubtime`, `yanked`, `cksum`), `cargo install` and project arguments, `CARGO_HOME`, the checks of one crate (a bad checksum, a missing one, a crate not in the index, a cached crate, a verdict already known), age (index time, API time, neither), the flows with a fake cargo (build, `--locked`, `--plan`, resolving commands, install, a block that restores the files), and `RealCargoTests` against the real cargo. **Mutation**: `cargosrc.py` 295 mutants, 291 killed; the four survivors were read: two `while True` loops that end by their own `break` either way, a line filter in the index reader that only saves work (`if version not in line`), and the one extra byte of the configuration-size read. `guard.py` (Cargo section): 289 mutants in the Cargo section: 254 killed at first, 35 survived. Seven are equivalent, or are limits that a test outside the fast set pins (the member, index-size and download-pattern constants, a computation only a resolving command could use, an empty file's write, the offline registry's flag); 28 were real gaps, in 18 places: a failed `cargo build` or `cargo install` that still reported the crates it had unpacked, a `cargo metadata` answer that is no mapping, the download host a registry's `config.json` names, crates.io's API host let through when crates.io is not in use, the API asked about another registry's crate, a lock that could not be read before a command, the `--offline` flag of the scratch resolution, the output of a failed resolution, the files put back on an interrupt, a registry's crates also reported as unchecked, the `installed` flag of `--json` in six paths, the longest download pattern, and the fetcher's close. Thirteen tests were added (125 in all), each shown to fail against its mutant; the 52 mutants on those lines were run again: 51 killed, and the one left is equivalent.
  - **Not covered**: registry credentials for cargo (`credential-provider`, `CARGO_REGISTRIES_*_TOKEN`); a git dependency's code; the content scan of `.rs` files and the `build.rs` and proc-macro detectors need the Rust lexer and `rsparse` wired (S-4).

- **V-1, stage 1: live secret verification, as a mechanism** (`0a23fc0`; `scanner/secretverify.py`, `secretverify_providers.py`, `secretverify_http.py`, `sigv4.py` (new); tests `tests/scanner/test_secretverify.py` 88, `test_secretverify_http.py` 55, `test_sigv4.py`, `tests/architecture/test_verifylive_script.py`; `scripts/verifylive/`; two fuzz targets; `_verify_stub.py`). John's request: a test that finds out whether a found secret is live, and does nothing that changes anything. **Nothing calls it yet**: stage 2 is `lazaret scan --verify-secrets`, after the tag and with S-4, and waits for John's decision 6 in the work split (proposed: P1, the 0.1.9 lane). Python only.
  - **The call only authenticates.** Seven providers, each with the call that says who the key belongs to and costs nothing: GitHub `GET /user`, Slack `auth.test`, Stripe `GET /v1/balance`, npm `whoami`, OpenAI and Anthropic `GET /v1/models`, AWS STS `GetCallerIdentity` (signed with SigV4: AWS's own test vectors in `test_sigv4.py`). **Outcomes are `live`, `rejected` and `unknown`, and a failed call is never `rejected`**: a timeout, a rate limit, a 5xx, no network, a budget spent and an answer no rule of the table matches are `unknown`, and the finding keeps its severity. Google API keys, JWTs and private keys have no identity call and are not verified (the report says why); databases are out of scope.
  - **What it promises, tested**: the host a secret goes to is in the provider table and nowhere else (not in scanned text, not in an answer, not in an argument); https on 443 only; **a redirect is never followed**; the secret goes in a header (AWS: only a signature) and **never in a URL**; it is sent only when it matches the provider's pattern in full (printable characters, so a crafted string cannot add a header); nothing returned holds the credential (a part that appears in a `detail` or `who` is replaced); the verdict cache is per run and keyed by provider, endpoint and the SHA-256 of the credential (TruffleHog's issue 3984 was one answer for two detectors), and the credential is never written anywhere; a time budget, a call budget, at most `per_provider` calls at once and a least interval; `HTTPS_PROXY` through an `http://` CONNECT tunnel (an `https://` proxy is refused, not bypassed); an answer is read to 64 KiB under one deadline kept by a watchdog; hostile answers (huge, not UTF-8, not JSON, JSON nested to the stack's limit, a `who` of control characters) never raise.
  - **The table is data, checked when it loads** (`validate`): a path or a query holds no part, a header or a body names only parts the entry has, a signed request sends no secret, every rule has a status list and an outcome. A new provider is a table entry and its tests.
  - **Provider answers are from the providers' documentation, not recorded** (the build has no route to them). `scripts/verifylive/verifylive.py` is the manual check: for each provider it asks about a made-up key in the provider's format (which must be `rejected`) and about the one in the environment (which must be `live`); nothing is sent without `--yes`. **Run it with real or test keys before a release that ships `--verify-secrets`**; a line marked `<-- not what was expected` means the table is wrong or the provider changed.
  - **Two fuzz targets** (`verify-answers`, `verify-credentials`; 34 in all): the first holds the outcome rules (an outcome from the three; `live` or `rejected` only where a rule of the table has that status; printable bounded `detail` and `who` with no part of the credential; the same answer twice), the second which credentials are sent and to where, with a reader that breaks each.
  - **Stage 2** (waits for the tag and decision 6): `--verify-secrets` on `lazaret scan`, off by default, never in `guard`, the registry auditor or the MCP server; an issue gains `verified: live | rejected | unknown`, and a `live` secret outranks the rest in the report; **`github:` and `gitlab:` scans refuse it unless the user says the repository is theirs** (a scan of someone else's repository must not become a way to test its secrets); the providers that will be called are named before the first call. A new spec, `specs/lazaret-secret-verification-stage1-2026-10-03.md`, has the design as built and the plan.
  - **Mutation**: `secretverify.py` 383 mutants, 374 killed; of the 9 survivors, 8 are equivalent (an empty text that no pattern matches (twice), the decoded-text cache, a document that is no mapping, the rate limiter's no-interval shortcut, an identity key that is the same either way, `or 1` for an empty list, and a cap that is only a cap) and one was a gap (a request for one worker could run two at a time), now held by a test. `sigv4.py`: 10 of 10 killed. `secretverify_http.py`: 154 mutants, 122 killed at first; the 32 survivors held real gaps (no test at the edge of the header, path and host limits, none of the transport refusing a bad request itself, none of a server that goes quiet midway being cut off at the deadline, none of the proxy settings being read from the process, of a request the connection cannot form, or of the timer being cancelled), and 11 tests were added. The survivors' lines were run again: 56 mutants, 45 killed, 11 left, each read: the default certificate context (`http.client` makes the same one), a thread's daemon flag, `while True` made true, four deadline checks that the watchdog makes redundant, an SSL error mapping that Python 3.13 does not reach, `conn.close()` (3.13's `http.client` closes on its own errors; 3.10's does not, so the call stays), and three read-size constants (the body is cut to its limit anyway).

- **R-2, the Rust item reader** (`rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/rsparse/`: `mod.rs`, `tree.rs`, `parser.rs`, `hooks.rs`, `out.rs`, `tests.rs`; the example `examples/rsparse_dump.rs`; `scripts/rsparse/`: `rustc_items.py`, `diff.py`, `mutate.py`, `README.md`; tests `tests/architecture/test_rsparse_scripts.py`, 46). New files and one additive `pub mod rsparse;` in `lib.rs`; **nothing calls it yet** (the detectors' registration in `api.rs` comes after the tag).
  - **Items, not Rust.** It finds `fn`, `struct`, `enum`, `union`, `trait`, `impl`, `mod`, `use` (one leaf per name brought in), `extern crate`, `extern { … }`, `static`, `const`, `type`, `macro_rules!` and macro calls in item position, with visibility, qualifiers (`const`, `async`, `unsafe`, `safe`, `extern "ABI"`, `default`, `auto`, a negative impl), outer and inner attributes and spans in code points, the braces of a body, and the parent; items in bodies, blocks, closures and initializers are found too and marked `LOCAL`. What is inside a body stays tokens (`Tree::toks` with `Tree::mate` for the delimiter pairs); a macro's tokens are never read as code. **The groups in an item's head and in a `struct`, `enum` or `union` body are read for the items they hold** (`[u8; { struct S; 1 }]`, `Foo<{ fn f() {} 1 }>`, `A = { fn f() {} 1 }`: the compiler's parser sees an item in each, and a `#[ctor]` could hide in one).
  - **No recursion, no `unsafe`, no panic**: a stack of frames on the heap holds the groups still to be read (`deep_nesting_costs_no_stack`: 200,000 deep, five shapes), and every token is looked at a bounded number of times (3,000 seeded soups of tokens: no panic, every item inside its parent and in order). A text that is not Rust gives what could be read and a count in `Tree::problems`.
  - **Held to `rustc`** (`scripts/rsparse/README.md`): `RUSTC_BOOTSTRAP=1 rustc -Zunpretty=ast-tree` is the oracle; `diff.py` compares kind, name, start, end, visibility, qualifiers, ABI, attributes, parent and the `use` leaves, file by file. On every `.rs` file of 186 crates in a cargo registry cache (7,468 files, 63 MB, 325,606 items): **the same items in all of them, none refused by `rustc`**, and the reader takes 0.8 s for the lot. On 18,000 broken variants of those files (`mutate.py`; two seeds): `rustc` still parses 6,646, **the reader finds the same items in every one**, and `rustc` refuses the other 11,354. One variant differed at first, an `extern "…"` ABI string with a DEL character: the two sides wrote the string differently (the comparison, not the reader), so the ABI is now one word on each side (`%XX`) and compared as the string it holds.
  - **What the corpus found** (in the order it found them): the oracle's faults (raw identifiers, file names with parentheses, `::`-rooted attribute paths, a `Cargo.toml` with no edition is 2015); then, in 24 files, the items inside the groups of an item's head and of a field list. All fixed, each a test in `rsparse/tests.rs` (46 tests).
  - **The scripts are mutation-checked**: `diff.py` 200 mutants, `mutate.py` 112 and `rustc_items.py` 425, all 737 killed by `test_rsparse_scripts.py`.
  - **Hooks** (`rsparse/hooks.rs`; code that runs without being called): `#[proc_macro]`, `#[proc_macro_derive]` and `#[proc_macro_attribute]` (the compiler runs them), `#[ctor]` and `#[dtor]` in any path, `#[link_section = ".init_array" | ".ctors" | "__DATA,__mod_init_func" | …]` (what the loader runs), a library's own `#[no_mangle] extern "C" fn main`-style entry, and `main` of a `build.rs` (given `build_script`). Each has the span of the item, so a rule can look for what the code does in that span and nowhere else. Nothing registers them yet.
  - **Not done**: an item inside an expression in an attribute's argument (`#[doc = { fn f() {} }]`) is not read (a documented gap; no corpus file has one); no `fuzz_rsparse` example (the differential run with `mutate.py` is the fuzzer, and needs `rustc`); the detectors on top of the hooks and their registration.

- **Go hooks** (`goparse/hooks.rs`, 11 tests; the same `pub use` in `goparse/mod.rs`; nothing registers them yet): `hooks(tree, src, comments) -> Vec<Hook>` lists Go code that runs without being called: `func init()` (every program that imports the package runs it), a package-level `var` whose initializer calls something other than a conversion or a built-in (`var x = f()`; a call inside a function literal counts only when the literal is itself called), the cgo preamble before `import "C"` (C the builder compiles, with the flags its `#cgo` lines give; a C constructor runs at start), `//go:linkname` (a name bound to one in another package, the runtime's included) and `//go:generate` (run by `go generate` only: its kind says `runs_without_a_command() == false`). It reads names and shapes, not what the code does; each hook has the span of the code that runs. The rule that reads the span and the registration wait for S-4.

- **P-16, part 1: every pattern on linre** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/linre/`: `hir.rs`, `looks.rs`, `nfa.rs`, `mod.rs`, `tests.rs`; `rxutil.rs` with `rxutil_tests.rs`, `signs.rs`, `strarr.rs`, `pyre/mod.rs`, `api.rs`; the rule pack, rule set 2.28.0; `docs/RUST_ENGINE.md` §14). The pack's 666 patterns, its lexers' tables included, and the patterns the engine builds as it scans run on linre; `pyre::Regex::new` fails on one linre would not run, and a built one that linre would not run is noted (`linre.fallbacks`, `linre.refused` since part 2), which fails the recorded-output runs. Lookaheads of unbounded width run with a memo per text, and a lookahead whose walks meet many sets is swept once (one pass from the window's end); a backreference to a one-character group of uncased characters runs as branches. Ten patterns or pieces that named something twice or counted past what a program holds are rewritten, eight with the check in code; SC-EVAL-DECODER and `_PERSIST_WRITE_RE` read further. A taint configuration's patterns still fall back to sre's matcher. Part 2: pyre's parser, prefilters and set patterns replaced by linre's, and the taint configuration's patterns held to linre; then the shlex port retired.

- **P-16, part 2: pyre's port of sre retired** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/pyre.rs` (was `pyre/`), `scan.rs` (was `pyre/scan.rs`), `linre/mod.rs`, `linre/literal.rs`, `rxutil.rs`, `api.rs`, `scanfile.rs`; `rust/NOTICE`; `python/src/lazaret/scanner/taintspec.py`; tests `linre/tests.rs`, `rxutil_tests.rs`, `test_linre_e.py` (new), `test_rust_parity_regex.py`, `test_linre_d.py`, `test_linre_linear.py`, `test_rust_notices.py`, `test_review_taint_config.py`). pyre is `re`'s interface to linre (450 lines; the port was 5,650); its parser, compiler, matcher, first-character tables and literal analysis are gone, and with them the SRE library's notices (Secret Labs AB, CNRI) from `rust/NOTICE`. scan_file's per-line gates read linre's strings. A built pattern linre refuses fails its call closed (SC-TRUNCATED; `linre.refused`), and a taint configuration's pattern linre refuses is a rejected rule. The Rust tests that compared linre with sre's matcher compare it with the answers recorded from it (a digest per test), and `test_linre_e.py` compares the same shapes with `re`. Still CPython-derived: the shlex port, the Final_Sigma rule and the casefix table.

- **P-16, part 3: the engine is Lazaret's own** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/hooks.rs`, `unicode.rs`, `generated/unicode13.rs`; `scripts/make_rust_tables.py`; `rust/NOTICE`, `rust/Cargo.toml`; tests `hooks.rs`'s and `unicode.rs`'s, `test_shell_words.py` (new), `test_rust_notices.py`). `shlex_split` is written from shlex's documentation (POSIX mode's parsing rules, `punctuation_chars`) as a plain scan, and `test_shell_words.py` holds it to Python's shlex on the hooks corpus and 30,000 random commands. The Final_Sigma rule follows the Unicode Standard's definition as `str.lower()` reads it (a character both cased and case-ignorable is passed over). `re`'s extra case equivalences are derived from Unicode's case mappings (the lowercase letters that share an uppercase; Unicode 13.0's letters on any Python), and `--check` holds them to `re`'s own table; the generated table is byte for byte the same. `rust/NOTICE` lists no translated file and says what was CPython's; the crates are `Apache-2.0 AND Unicode-3.0`. The packages keep `LICENSE-PYTHON` and `Python-2.0.1` until the CPython codec names they hold are settled (the npm package's `codecs.js`, the dashboard's copy).

- **P-1, part 4: fewer tries, a tighter automaton** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/linre/prefilter.rs`, `linre/mod.rs`, `linre/dfa.rs`, `textgate.rs`, `signs.rs`). A pattern's lead strings are kept up to 30% of positions stopped at (40% for a program over 2,000 instructions; it was 10% and 20%): each stop is checked for a whole string before a try, and over a long text the places are the pairs'. A first-character scan is kept only up to one character in twenty-five (it was one in eight): past that a try at each place costs more than the DFA's pass. The places of up to 16 pairs are merged without a heap; the forward DFA's inner loop runs over a slice, two characters a round; the import-time test reads only the rows that hold `curl` or `wget` for a download piped into a shell (the test asks that first). In instructions, each file after a warm engine (each bundle after the other), from `next` before P-1: `proxy_server.py` 1,660 M to 1,067 M (-36%), `coreBundle.js` 6,494 M to 3,372 M (-48%), `utilsBundle.js` 9,421 M to 4,979 M (-47%); part 4's own share is 4%, 21% and 21%.

- **No package carries CPython's license** (Oct 4; John's answer to the open question P-16 part 3 left: the codec names are facts; `js/LICENSE-PYTHON` and `rust/LICENSE-PYTHON` removed; `rust/NOTICE`, `js/NOTICE`, `js/package.json`, `python/_build/lazaret_build.py`, `scripts/check_native_library.py`; tests `test_notices.py`, `test_rust_notices.py`, `test_build_backend.py`, `test_native_library_check.py`, `test_review_build_hygiene.py`, `test_review_npm_pack.py`, `js/test/architecture.test.js`). The wheels, the sdist and the npm package are `Apache-2.0 AND Unicode-3.0`, as the crates are: the codec names `src/lib/codecs.js` and the dashboard list are what Python's codecs answer to, not CPython's code. `check_native_library.py --dist` fails a release that names a License-File it does not ship, and the notice tests fail if a CPython license file, notice or expression comes back.

- **R-1, part 1: the Rust reader in the engine** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/rsread/` (new: `mod.rs`, `ast.rs`, `val.rs`, `eval.rs`, `facts.rs`, `tests.rs`), `signs.rs`, `api.rs` (`rs_crate`), `docs/RUST_ENGINE.md` §21). A crate's `.rs` code read for what it does when it is built (the build script and a procedural-macro crate: the install test, CRITICAL), when it starts (`#[ctor]`, load sections, a `no_mangle extern fn main`, `#![no_main]`: the import-time test) and when it is used (the rest: the import-time test, strong reasons only). The reading evaluates the code — `ast.rs` reads a function body's statements and expressions from `rsparse`'s tokens, `val.rs` is a value (the text it builds, the data it carries, the handle it is), `eval.rs` follows the crate's own functions, closures and methods and records processes started, data sent, files written then run, names looked up, and `facts.rs` turns those into the facts the install-script and import-time tests ask (`signs::ModelFacts`). Scope's narrower plan (John, Oct 4): Rust's build-time code first. **Nothing calls it yet** (the Go reader and the registry and guard wiring follow). The two tests now take a model's facts only when one is given, so Python's and JavaScript's answers are unchanged: the benchmark (945) and the popular set (1,205) are identical to `next`'s, the ten recorded snapshots pass, and the engine's tests (245, with 28 for the reader) pass. John asked whether Go and Rust are worth going on with; the answer (yes, narrowed) is in `specs/lazaret-go-rust-parity-plan-2026-10-04.md` and the backlog's row 10.

- **The Go/Rust review's fixes** (Oct 4; John asked whether the 0.1.9 agent's Go and Rust work had been reviewed; it had not been, line by line, so it was: four reviews (`guard go`, `guard cargo`, the registry's Go and crates modules, the SCA inventory), each finding run before it was fixed; the review and what is left are `audits/lazaret-go-rust-code-review-2026-10-04.md` in the project). `registry/guard.py`, `cargosrc.py`, `goproxy.py`, `repo.py`, `ecosystems/golang.py`; `scanner/programs.py` (new), `hook.py`, `sca.py`, `gomod.py`, `sca_feeds.py`; the rule set 2.29.0. Fixed: a crate's files in tar entries of an unusual type, which cargo unpacks, were left out unread (RM-1: SC-ARCHIVE-TYPE); the guard's scratch projects in the shared `/tmp` (CG-1); `cargo install`'s graph, unpacked crates, cargo's configuration, unread sources as notes (CG-2 to CG-6, CG-9); the local Go proxy open to the machine, a second unscanned fetch, the module cache and `GONOPROXY` modules never checked, `go env`'s toolchain download, `-C`/`GOFLAGS`/program arguments, `GOPRIVATE` read as `GONOPROXY`, programs found in the current folder, unknown release times (GO-2 to GO-9); and SCA-1 to SCA-7 (workspaces, unknowns kept, Go's escapes and long versions, every go.sum version, unused vendor replacements, each ecosystem's freshness, the replace lookup). **Left for John** (decision): GO-1, a module or crate whose code is not read is INCOMPLETE and passes by default, so Go-code malware and a payload padded past the size limit pass; the review recommends blocking truncated code files by default and taking SC-UNREAD-CODE out of `--block-warn`. Recorded, not fixed: CG-7, CG-8, GO-10, RM-3 and the rest in the review's backlog.

- **The readers share their values, events and facts** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/model/` (new: `mod.rs`, `val.rs`, `events.rs`, `facts.rs`), `rsread/lit.rs` (was `val.rs`), `rsread/`). What Rust's reader records moves to `crate::model` so that the Go reader (`go_package`, next) uses the same copy; nothing else changes.
- **The Rust reader's review** (Oct 4; the Rust reader of R-1's first part had not been reviewed line by line, and the Go reader was to be built on it, so it was, each finding run on the old code first: `rsread/ast.rs`, `eval.rs`, `mod.rs`, `model/facts.rs`, tests `rsread/tests.rs` (18 new); part 2 of `audits/lazaret-go-rust-code-review-2026-10-04.md`). Fixed: input that made the reader recurse without a bound overflowed the stack and aborted the engine (RR-1, RR-2: about 200 KB of `a = a = …`, of `else if`s, of nested patterns or function types, of `.f()` or `+`); events, closures and copied text were bounded only by steps (RR-3, RR-4: 180,000 events from one test); a response read with no argument panicked (RR-5); `.unwrap()` dropped a response, so `get(u).unwrap().copy_to(&mut f)` wrote nothing (RR-9); a build script's payload 9 calls deep, or behind code that spends the reading's steps, was never read, and with a model the text test does not look for a download piped into a shell (RR-10: every function of a build script and a procedural-macro crate is read, every function a start-up function calls by name, and a reading cut short is read by the text test too); `cfg(all(feature = "x", test))` code was read as the library's (RR-11); and two signs were wider than the text detectors' (RR-7: a DNS lookup of the machine's own name or a local domain; RR-8: a connection as the stdio of any program, not a shell). Nothing calls `rs_crate` yet, so no scan's answer changes.
- **G-1, part 1: the Go reader in the engine** (Oct 4; `rust/crates/lazaret-engine/src/goread/` (new: `mod.rs`, `eval.rs`, `lit.rs`, `cread.rs`, `tests.rs`), `model/val.rs` (Go's handles), `model/events.rs` (a script read command by command; certutil, bitsadmin and `Invoke-WebRequest` downloads), `api.rs` (`go_package`), `docs/RUST_ENGINE.md` §22). A module's `.go` code read for what it does when a program that imports its packages starts (`init`, package initializers, a cgo constructor: the import-time test) and when used (the rest: strong reasons only), on `goparse`'s trees; `cread.rs` reads a cgo package's C for the programs it runs. The documented Go techniques are found on inert samples (the typosquats' string arrays, Linux and Windows; DNS TXT commands in an init goroutine; base64 commands; download, chmod and run; a fetched wiper script; a reverse shell; a cgo constructor; credentials and the environment sent at load and at use); Go's standard library and its vendored modules give no finding (717 folders, 6,121 files). Built with the Rust reader's review's bounds from the start. **Nothing calls it yet.** Scope: John's narrower order, the code Go's `init` reaches (look-alike module paths next, then the wiring). John kept the C reader (Oct 4).
- **N-3, Go: look-alike module paths** (Oct 4; `registry/lookalike.py` (`go_path`, `go_tables`, `go_lookalike`, and `issues` for `go`), `registry/popular_names.json` (`go`: 4,893 module paths), `registry/repo.py` (`_lookalike_names` reads the `go.mod` in a module zip's root), `scripts/update-popular-names.py` (`--go`); tests `tests/registry/test_lookalike.py`, 12 new). SC-TYPOSQUAT (MAJOR) for a module's own path and the paths its `go.mod` requires, compared part by part: the owner one change from a well-known module's (`shopsprint/decimal`), its separators changed, or a word of `GO_AFFIXES` added (`boltdb-go/bolt`; taking one away was dropped with Part C: Shopify/sarama's `go.mod` requires `github.com/xdg/scram`, the old home of `xdg-go/scram`), the repository the same; the host one change, the rest the same; a gopkg.in name one change. Never an owner or host of a well-known module, a module of the module's own owner, or a two- or three-character owner with a repository under five characters; another owner's module of the same repository name is a fork. **The list:** no Go registry publishes downloads and proxy.golang.org, pkg.go.dev and deps.dev cannot be reached from here, so it is the modules awesome-go lists (MIT; the first link of each entry outside its Resources and Editor Plugins) and Debian packages (the `Go-Import-Path` of Ubuntu 24.04's main and universe indices; module paths only), lower-cased, without a major version. **Measured:** of the 4,893, two would be flagged were they not listed (`pions/webrtc`, pion's old name; `gopkg.in/macaron.v1`, beside `macaroon.v1`): two real pairs, both listed; Go 1.24's `go.mod` files and vendored modules, with the module paths in the test data (404 paths in all), give none. The long tail (index.golang.org's million paths) can't be measured here; it is the Go benchmark's (N-2). **Not covered:** a random owner with a popular repository's name (`cavernouskina/mcp-go`, the 2025 reports' commonest shape): forks share it; the module's code is the guard's test there. `Go.popular_names()` stays None until X-2 routes the look-alike corpus through the modules.
- **Part C, first part: a module's and a crate's code read where they are scanned** (Oct 4, night; rule set 2.31.0; `registry/repo.py` (`PACKAGE_CODE`, `_package_code`, `_never_built`, `_go_mods`; `UNREAD_CODE` emptied), `scanner/engine.py` (`go_package`, `rs_crate`), `registry/ecosystems/crates.py` (`layout`), the engine's `api::call_owned` (the FFI hands a package's text over without a copy of each file); tests `tests/registry/test_package_code.py` (18, new), `test_unread_code.py` (rewritten: no artifact's code is left unread), the guard tests (a module whose init runs a download and a crate whose build script does are blocked; the clean fixtures are OK, not INCOMPLETE), `goread/tests.rs` (the owned call answers as the borrowing one)). The guard and the registry scan a `gomod` zip's `.go` files (with the cgo packages' C) and a `crate`'s `.rs` files with the file rules in dependency mode and the engine's readers: SC-IMPORT-RISK for what Go's init code reaches and a Rust `#[ctor]`, SC-INSTALL-HOOK (CRITICAL) for a build script and a procedural macro, SC-USE-RISK for the strong reasons of the rest, SC-GO-GENERATE (INFO) for `//go:generate`. Files no build of a dependent compiles are read by neither. **Measured** on Ubuntu 24.04's packaged Go modules and crates (`golang-*-dev`, `librust-*-dev`, read from their `.deb` files; only those whose every upstream licence is MIT/Expat, BSD, Apache-2.0, ISC or Zlib, 1,888 and 2,116 packages; the others were not opened), each packed as the proxy and crates.io pack it and scanned as the guard scans it: Go 1,953 modules, 1,912 OK, 39 WARN, 0 SUSPICIOUS, 2 INCOMPLETE (Debian's single trees of aws-sdk-go-v2 and azure-sdk-for-go, 344 and 513 million characters, over the 300 million a reader takes); Rust 2,120 crates, 2,090 OK, 30 WARN, 0 SUSPICIOUS. 100 s and 47 s. Every WARN was read: base64 and hex constants (stringer's name tables are all of 9 of Go's, and part of a 10th: G-5), test vectors in `src/`, archives, opaque blobs and binaries in fixtures, an npm manifest's install hook inside two crates (N-18), a doubled emoji variation selector in a doc comment (aes). Found on the way and fixed: rivo/uniseg was SUSPICIOUS for escaped URLs in its `_test.go` (files no dependent builds are not read now); Shopify/sarama's `go.mod` requires `github.com/xdg/scram`, the old home of `xdg-go/scram`, which N-3's look-alike taking a word away flagged (dropped); the Go reader held 3 GB for aws-sdk-go v1 and the sweep's first run was killed (the per-file copies removed; the 300 million cap). The 5 modules over 60 million characters were scanned one at a time: aws-sdk-go WARN, 11 s, 2.6 GB; google.golang.org/api OK, 10 s, 1.9 GB.
- **Part C, second part: `lazaret-registry go:… crates:…`** (Oct 4, night; `registry/repo.py` (`MODULE_ECOSYSTEMS`, `ECOSYSTEMS`, `registry_module`, `module_transport`, `_ModuleRedirects`, `module_fetch`, `_module_digest`; `parse_spec`, `_check_name`, `_check_version`, `resolve`, `scan_package` and `new_dependency_issues` route `go` and `crates` to their module), `registry/ecosystems/base.py` (doc), `mcp/server.py` (`scan_package`'s description); tests `tests/registry/test_registry_go_crates.py` (25, new: specs, resolving, scans of fnv 1.0.7 and go-difflib v1.0.0 from their recorded responses, a crate whose build script and a module whose init pipe a download into a shell, a swapped download of each never scanned, a sweep that stores, skips and goes on past a failure, the MCP tool, the transport's host rule, budget, status and redirects), `test_ecosystems_base.py` (the errors and `Resolution` are the same classes)). X-2's part for Part C: `repo.py` re-exports `SpecError`, `FetchError`, `DigestError` and `Resolution` from `base.py`, and supplies the modules' transport, `_fetch`'s bounded, timed read with the module's own URL rule (`Fetch.check_url`) for the URL and every redirect. `add`, `scan`, `scan-all`, `report` and the MCP tool take `go:<module>[@vX.Y.Z]` and `crates:<name>[@version]`; the module resolves (proxy `@latest`/`.info` and the checksum database; the sparse index), the download is checked by the module (`h1:`; the index's SHA-256) before anything is scanned, failing closed with SC-DIGEST-MISMATCH in npm's and PyPI's words, and `scan_package` scans it as the guard does. npm and PyPI keep their code (X-2's later steps move it). **Not done:** `discover` for either (decision 2 for crates.io), SC-NEW-DEPENDENCY for a crate (Part D), the modules' member rules in `iter_archive` (`root=`: the scan reads members as the guard does, by the `<module>@<version>` element), and a Go zip over the 200 MiB download limit is an error rather than INCOMPLETE (no size is declared before the download).
- **Part C, third part: the Rust crate inside a PyPI sdist (N-17), and the readers' files kept whole** (Oct 4, night; `registry/repo.py` (`_sdist_crates`, `_reader_findings` (the findings of a reader's answer, shared with `_package_code`), `_keep_code`, `code_text`; `member` keeps an sdist's `.rs` and `Cargo.toml` files and a module's cgo C for the readers, and counts a `.go`, `.rs` or cgo file over the member limit as not scanned); tests `tests/registry/test_package_code.py` (15 new: `SdistCrateTests`, the member limit for Go and Rust, cgo C kept past the text budget; ten mutations of the new code, each caught)). Each directory of an sdist with a `Cargo.toml` is a crate and a `.rs` file is in the nearest one above it (in none: cargo never builds it, not read); its tests/, benches/ and examples/ are left out as a `.crate`'s are. The file rules read each crate's `.rs` files, and the Rust reader each crate: a build script and a procedural macro are SC-INSTALL-HOOK (CRITICAL; pip has cargo build the crate when it installs the sdist), a `#[ctor]` SC-IMPORT-RISK (the extension module is loaded at import), the strong reasons of the rest SC-USE-RISK, the crates sharing one USE_RISK_CHARS (none once the sdist is SUSPICIOUS, as `_use_time_code`). A crate's file over the member limit, or past PACKAGE_CODE_CHARS, makes the sdist INCOMPLETE. **Found on the way (Part C's first part, fixed here):** a `.go` or `.rs` file over `--max-source-bytes` was classified as a non-source file and the module or crate could be OK with it unread; a module's cgo `.c`/`.h` files shared the 64 MiB text budget, and one past it was left out of the reader. **Measured** on 72 popular PyPI sdists with a crate (the top 5,000 projects whose metadata says Rust, and 34 known Rust-backed ones; MIT, BSD, Apache-2.0, ISC or Zlib only, by their metadata; 119 MB): before, 67 OK, 4 WARN, 1 SUSPICIOUS; after, 65 OK, 6 WARN, 1 SUSPICIOUS, 11 s to 28 s in all (uv 0.2 to 2.3 s), peak memory 749 MB (typos: 23 MB of generated tables, read by the reader). The two new WARNs were read: whenever's `DIGITS` table (200 digits, SC-B64: G-5's class) and rfc3161-client's hex test vectors in `#[test]` functions in `src/` (SC-B64; N-20); uv, WARN already for its Windows trampolines, gains an SC-B64 for a base64 test deploy key in its uv-test crate. The SUSPICIOUS one is not Rust: rumdl's maintainer scripts `scripts/update-used-by.py` and `release_assets.py` pass values to `gh` as arguments, in a file whose parser's description is its own `__doc__` (N-19). The npm and PyPI benchmarks hold no sdist with Rust; Ubuntu's packaged Go modules and crates scan as before (1,953 and 2,120, the same verdicts and findings).
- **Part C, fourth part: `--deps` reads a Go `vendor/` and a `cargo vendor` tree** (Oct 4, night; `scanner/core.py` (`_vendor_kind`, `_vendor_lang`, `never_built`, `cargo_layout`, `package_reader_issues` (the readers' findings, moved here from repo.py so both use them), `_vendored_code`, `_vendored_modules`, `_cgo_texts`; `_dep_tree_kind` and the walker carry a vendor tree's kind; `dependency_checks` calls the readers), `scanner/engine.py` (`cargo_layout`, `go_vendored_modules`), the engine's `vendor.rs` (a crate's `Cargo.toml` and a `vendor/modules.txt` read once for both packages; RUST_ENGINE.md §23), the rule pack (17 values: the readers' texts and limits, the vendor trees'), `registry/repo.py` (`never_built`, the findings and the limits from core), and the npm package's twins (`js/src/lib/fs.js` `vendorKind`, `neverBuilt`; `js/src/deps.js` `vendoredCode`, `crateLayout`, `packageReaderIssues`; `js/src/lib/native.js` `goPackage`, `rsCrate`, `cargoLayout`, `goVendoredModules`); tests `tests/scanner/test_vendored_code.py` (14, new), `js/test/vendored-code.test.js` (5, new), `tests/architecture/test_js_parity.py` (`test_vendored_code_agrees`: both CLIs on a tree with both kinds, with and without `--deps`), `vendor::tests` (8)). A vendor directory with a `modules.txt` (Go's marker already) or whose crate directories hold a regular `.cargo-checksum.json` is a vendor tree of that kind; with `--deps` each `.go` or `.rs` file a build compiles is read with the file rules in dependency mode (never-built files are not read: `*_test.go`, `_`/`.` names, `testdata/`; a crate's `tests/`, `benches/`, `examples/`), and each module `vendor/modules.txt` lists (a file of none: its package's directory) gets the Go reader with its packages' cgo `.c`/`.h` files, each crate the Rust reader with the engine's reading of its `Cargo.toml`. A `cargo vendor` tree is pruned without `--deps` now (it was read as the project's own code). **Measured:** the engine's reading of `Cargo.toml` against the registry's (Python's TOML) on the 2,122 crates of Ubuntu's licence-checked `librust-*-dev` packages: the same build script, library root and macro flag for every one. Not done: the use-time share is each module's or crate's own (24,000,000 characters), not the tree's; other dependency trees' Go and Rust files are not read.
- **A download piped into a shell named by its path** (Oct 4; rule set 2.30.0; `rules/lazaret-rules.json` and `scanner/core.py` (`_PIPE_SCAN_RE`), the dashboard's twin; tests `test_review_script_patterns.py`). Found while building the Go reader: `| /bin/bash`, `| /usr/bin/env sh` and `| sudo /usr/local/bin/zsh` were not read as a pipe into a shell, in any language (the 2025 Go typosquats' `wget -O - … | /bin/bash &`).

## Text for the files the 0.1.8 agent owned: moved (S-4, part 5, Oct 4)

README, CHANGELOG, STRUCTURE.md, `docs/DESIGN.md` (§5e to §5h and §10), `js/README.md` and `python/README.md` now carry the lane's work: S-1 with N-5, S-2's online half, the guards for Go and Cargo, Go and Rust in SCA and the indexed bundle, `--from-plan`, `--keepalive`, the scan workers, the parsers, the fuzzers and the profile scripts. Still waiting:

- **V-1**: no longer waiting. Its CHANGELOG entry and `docs/DESIGN.md` §5k came with stage 2's third step, which shipped `--verify-secrets`: off by default and never in `guard`, the registry auditor, the MCP server or the pre-commit hook; a failed call is `unknown`, never `rejected`; the secret goes in a header, never in a URL; no redirect is followed; the host comes only from the provider table; the cache is per run and keyed by provider, endpoint and the SHA-256 of the secret. Any target is verified, `github:` and `gitlab:` repositories that are not the user's included (John's decision 4, Oct 7, as TruffleHog does; the stage 1 plan had them refuse it).
- **`--help` of core** does not list `--accept-incomplete` or the `github:` form (core's parser).

## Wire-up (S-4): done, Oct 4

1. `api.rs` and `lexer.rs` take `go` and `rs` (part 1).
2. `.go` and `.rs` are routed to the engine in both walkers, a project's own files only (part 1; packages and dependency trees wait for the detectors).
3. `.cargo/credentials.toml` is read and its token found; a `.netrc` is read, but its `password value` lines are not found (a gap, in the backlog).
4. `docs/RUST_ENGINE.md` has the two lexers and the recorded outputs (part 1); CHANGELOG entries for every part (parts 1 to 5).
5. The snapshot corpus has a Go and Rust set (`go_rs_corpus`: curated files and 1,500 random ones; part 1); twenty popular crates and four directories of Go's standard library were scanned to set the duplication rule.
6. S-2's and S-3's offline checks are in both scanners (part 2), counted by the gate only when CRITICAL.
7. The check: the recorded snapshots, the benchmark and the popular set identical before and after each part (the new `SC-WORKFLOW-*` and `SC-GITLAB-*` ids aside); the holdout is read once, in aggregate, at the release gate.

Also in S-4: N-1 (part 3: a Go module or a crate with code is INCOMPLETE until the detectors exist) and N-5 (part 4).

- The fuzzer for the parsers (`js_parse`, `py_parse`) and the taint passes, on this harness: it takes a `Lang` (a lexer, a generator, an `is_space`), so another reading is one more entry in `langs()`; for the lexers of JavaScript and Python add their token generators.
- **N-19: code read back from the file itself, in Python, on the tree** (Oct 6; the engine's `pyflow/supply.rs` (`own_starts`, `K_OWN` given at the call whose callee starts a read of the script itself, or at `__doc__`/`__file__` where one starts; kept through a read of a path the script named (`open(p)`, `p.read_text()`, `Path(p)`), dropped out of `USAGE_PARSERS` (`argparse.ArgumentParser`, `optparse.OptionParser`, `docopt.docopt`); `Out::Own` where it reaches code run, in the function or through a callee; `Facts.own`, `own_run`), `jsflow/supply.rs` (`K_OWN`, `KIND_NAMES`, `NOT_LOCAL`), `jsflow/driver.rs` (`Out::Own`), `signs.rs` (`own_source_run_at`: the install-script and import-time tests ask the tree for a Python text, the text follower otherwise), rule set 2.32.0; tests `pyflow::supply::tests` (`own_code_run`, 11 forms; `own_not_code_run`, 7, rumdl's among them), `tests/scanner/test_supply_chain_signals.py` (`test_a_usage_text_and_a_program_given_arguments`)). rumdl 0.2.78 was SUSPICIOUS for two maintainer scripts (`scripts/update-used-by.py`, `scripts/release_assets.py`): each gives argparse its docstring as the usage text and runs `subprocess.run(["gh", *args])` in a function whose parameter is `args`. `runs_own_source_at` follows names across the file, so `main()`'s `args = parser.parse_args()` was `run_gh`'s parameter, and any `subprocess` call a code runner. On the tree a program given arguments runs that program (a spawn runs code only with `shell=True`, a shell's line, or an interpreter's `-c`), scopes are the language's, and a usage text's parser gives the command line. A Python text the tree can't read (it doesn't parse, it is over 2 MB, or it passes the pass's budgets) and JavaScript keep the text follower; so does the install-script test of a text given no language.
- **Part D: crate names (N-3's second part) and SC-NEW-DEPENDENCY for a crate** (Oct 6; `registry/lookalike.py` (crates: `normalize` folds case and `_` as `-`, the `crates` tables, the Cargo.toml lines a finding points at; `_line_of` puts a needle that starts with a line break on the line after it), `registry/repo.py` (`_lookalike_names` reads a crate's `Cargo.toml` through the crates module's `declared`; `crates_new_dependencies`, `new_dependency_issues` for crates), `registry/ecosystems/crates.py` (`records`: the index's versions with their `pubtime`, kept by `_reduce`; `owners`: crates.io's API, its only use, at one request a second (`rate`); the API host declared), `popular_names.json` (`crates`: 5,000 targets, 373 known), `scripts/fetch-top-crates.py` (new: the API's two lists, 200 pages each, the most it pages through), `scripts/update-popular-names.py` (`--crates PAGES_DIR [UBUNTU_SOURCES…]`, `crates_section`, `debian_crates`); tests `tests/registry/test_lookalike.py` (`CratesLookalikeTests`, 5; the script's crates section), `tests/registry/test_registry_go_crates.py` (`NewCrateDependencyTests`, 4), `tests/registry/test_crates.py` (the API host and rate, `pubtime` kept or not)). - **G-5 and N-23: two false positives of the file rules in Go and Rust code** (Oct 6; rule set 2.35.0; the engine's `scanfile.rs` (`b64_blob_col` and `b64_plain`: the first run on a line that can be base64 data; `hidden_unicode_run` passes over a presentation selector repeated), the pack's `_B64_PLAIN_MAX` (16,384), `_B64_PERIOD_MAX` (64), `_PRESENTATION_SELECTORS` (U+FE0E, U+FE0F) and `_PRESENTATION_REPEAT_MAX` (4), the dashboard's twins (`b64Plain`, `b64BlobCol`, `hiddenPresentationRepeat`); tests `test_review_b64_blobs.py` (new), `test_review_hidden_unicode.py`, `test_review_dashboard_parity.py` (`b64_plain.js`, `b64_bound.js`, `hidden_unicode_emoji.js`), the corpus's `b64_plain` cases (the snapshots re-recorded: only SC-B64 and SC-HIDDEN-UNICODE lines moved), the npm package's `review-hidden-unicode`, `detection` and `cli` tests; the fixtures that wrote base64 as `"QUJD" * n`, `"Zm9v" * n` or `"A" * 250` use base64 data now: `B64_DATA`, base64 of 240 bytes, and the corpus's of 165). SC-B64 passes over a run of digits, hex digits (after `0x` or not) or letters alone, up to 16,384 characters, and a period of up to 64 characters repeated, and reports the first run after them on the line that can be data; SC-HIDDEN-UNICODE passes over U+FE0E or U+FE0F repeated up to four times. The bound is the benchmark's: without it, three malicious PyPI releases (forgyp 1.0.3, forgyps 2.0.0, readycharz 0.2.0) lost SC-B64 on a hex payload of 276,592 to 301,848 digits (their verdicts held, SUSPICIOUS for other findings), while the longest one-class run of the benign sets is 9,327 digits (num-bigint-dig's tests) and of hex 6,618 (openssl). Measured on Part F's sets, the popular set and the benchmark: the popular crates 20 → 17 WARN (aes, aws-lc-rs, crc), Go's own 4 → 3 (the vendored x/tools), Ubuntu's Go modules 39 → 27 and its crates 30 → 22 (7 SC-B64, and aes 0.8.3), the popular npm and PyPI releases 36 → 32 (monaco-editor, prettier, modal, notebook), and on the benchmark one benign release (google-adk) and two benign releases' weak finding (dash, inspect-ai); no malicious verdict moves, and the three hex payloads are SC-B64 as before. What SC-B64 still reports on the Go and Rust sets is base64 that is data (keys and host keys, certificates, test messages, btcd's precomputed table) and `stringer` tables of mixed case (six Ubuntu modules, and std, cmd and x/arch). A statistic of the letters' case was looked at for those tables: the share of adjacent letters of the same case is at least 0.549 in them and at most 0.484 in the benchmark's malicious runs, but base64 of a binary with many zero bytes reaches 0.60 (benign WebAssembly), so it is not used (backlog G-5).
- **N-18: an npm manifest inside a crate or a Go module** (Oct 6; rule set 2.36.0; `registry/repo.py` (`_npm_never_installs`, called on what `scan_manifest` and `scan_gyp` find in a Go module or a crate and on the implicit `node-gyp rebuild` of a crate's root `binding.gyp`); tests `test_package_code.py` (`NpmInsideTests`: a plain hook, a `binding.gyp` action and the implicit rebuild listed as INFO, in a crate and in a Go module; a hostile command, gyp action or hook script still CRITICAL; an npm package's hooks unchanged)). npm never installs a package from inside a crate or a Go module, so a plain hook there is inventory (INFO, with that reason); a hostile command stays CRITICAL, and the script a hook names is still followed (a build script can run npm there). Ubuntu's crates 22 → 20 WARN (insta 1.34.0's editor extension, tree-sitter-cli 0.20.8's npm wrapper); no other set moves. A Go module's root `package.json` is not at the root as the scan names members (`m@v1.0.0/package.json`, N-21), so a module gets no implicit rebuild. A crate's root `package.json` still gives its `main` and `exports` the import-time test, as an npm package's: nothing on the sets comes of it.
- **N-20: a dependency's Rust test items left out of the file rules** (Oct 6; rule set 2.37.0; the engine's `scanfile.rs` (`rs_test_only_lines`, applied in `scan_file`, which is dependency mode only), `rsread/mod.rs` (`test_items` and `cfg_only_in_tests`, shared with it); tests `test_review_rust_test_items.py` (new), `test_package_code.py` (`RustTests`), the npm package's `vendored-code` test, and the Go/Rust corpus's `test_items.rs` case (the `go_rs` snapshots re-recorded: the new engine gives the 1,550 earlier outputs unchanged, and the new case's two are the only change)). The items the Rust reader takes as tests (`#[test]`, `#[bench]`, a path ending in `::test`, a `cfg` true only in tests: `test` or `all(…, test)`), an item whose own inner attributes hold such a `cfg`, or the whole file under its `#![cfg(test)]`: a line is left out only when all of its text, blanks aside, is in one, and a file with a parse problem (a delimiter without its mate, an item cut short) leaves none out. A mutation pass (7 mutants, each built) caught all but one, the dependency-mode check, which nothing can reach: project mode is `scan_rules`. Measured: the popular crates 17 → 16 WARN (rustls 0.23.45), Ubuntu's crates 20 → 16 (charset 0.1.3, dns-parser 0.8.0, mailparse 0.14.0, rustls 0.21.10), Go's sets unchanged. Left: doc-test examples in `///` comments (rsa 0.9.2's `oaep.rs`), and a module file that another file declares under `#[cfg(test)] mod tests;` (the file rules read a file at a time).

Found by the Go reader's tests (`~/.ssh/id_ed25519` read and posted was a finding, `~/.aws/credentials` was not); the same held in JavaScript and Python, and a store named in a joined path (`path.join(os.homedir(), '.aws', 'credentials')`) or at the end of one over 60 characters was missed too. - **NET-1, part 1: tiny_https in the repository, and the native transport the registry's default** (Oct 6, decision 12; `rust/crates/tiny_https` (new: the library as it was handed over, 305 files; `LAZARET.md`, `vendored.sha256`, `.gitattributes`), `scripts/sync_tiny_https.py` (new: takes a drop, refusing links, devices, absolute or `..` paths and a manifest with dependencies; tests `lazaret-net`'s 14 against tiny_https's TLS 1.3 and HTTP/2 test server, `lazaret-verify`'s 2 on the real checksum-database capture, `tests/scanner/test_nativenet.py` (new: 15, an HTTPS server of Python's `ssl` on 127.0.0.1 with a root made by `openssl`; a TLS 1.2 server left to urllib), `test_rust_deps.py` (the line between the engine and the network), `test_build_backend.py` (the sdist's tiny_https sources), `test_native_library_check.py` (an export trie of eight names); the four modules that fake urllib's opener take Python's transport (`_support.PYTHON_TRANSPORT`)). Measured from here against the real registries, fresh processes, 3 to 7 runs: 200 npm documents by 16 threads 8.3 s over urllib, 0.64–0.91 s over the new HTTP/1.1 and 0.61–0.68 s over its HTTP/2; 100 npm tarballs by 8 threads 2.85, 0.40 and 0.43 s; one 47 MB wheel a median 0.35 s over HTTP/1.1 and 0.55 s over HTTP/2; six wheels at once (58 MB) 0.26 and 0.69 s. So documents (a budget of at most 32 MiB) are offered h2 and downloads go over HTTP/1.1 (`LAZARET_HTTP`). tiny_https's own offline tests pass in the workspace (1,133 unit tests, go_vectors, cms_vectors, sigstore_real, sigstore_synthetic, rekor_real), the native library checks for the five release targets, and the WebAssembly build links none of it. Live from here: npm, PyPI and files.pythonhosted.org over HTTP/2, crates.io's index and static.crates.io; the Go proxy answers 403 from here to either transport. Not yet: the guard's fetcher and `keepalive.py`, `sources.py`, the SCA feeds and secret verification (part 2), the checksum database check (part 3). The library has not run on Windows or ARM hardware before (its B-58); CI's jobs there are its first.
- **NET-1, part 2: the guard's downloads, the sources and the SCA feeds on the native transport** (Oct 6; `registry/guard.py` (`Fetcher._open_native`: an https request without credentials, the fetcher's hosts and the URL's own as the rule, or no host rule where `https_redirects` lets a redirect go to any https host, no proxy for this machine; `_NativeResponse`: `headers.get` in any case, `read`, TooLarge over the budget; `Fetcher.open`'s `max_bytes`, given by `fetch` and `fetch_to_file`; the pip index's relay), `registry/sources.py` (`_http`: a request without a token), `scanner/sca_feeds.py` (`_fetch_native`: a feed's download, any https host, `MAX_REDIRECTS`), `scanner/nativenet.py` (`hosts=None` for no host rule, `proxy` for a caller's choice), `lazaret-net` (`Request::any_host`; the URL limits still hold) and the C ABI's `any_host`; tests `tests/registry/test_guard_native_fetch.py` (new: a fetch, a download to a file, TooLarge, the hosts on a redirect and any https host with `https_redirects`, a status, credentials left to urllib), `test_nativenet.py` (the request without a host rule), `lazaret-net`'s URL-rule test (no host rule, and http still refused)). What stays on urllib, and why: plain http (a registry served on this machine), and every request with credentials (a private registry's token or a URL's `user:password@` in the guard, `GITHUB_TOKEN`, `GITLAB_TOKEN`, and secret verification): urllib's redirect hook gives a hop its own host's credentials and takes GitLab's `PRIVATE-TOKEN` off another host, which tiny_https cannot (it drops `Authorization`, `Cookie` and `Proxy-Authorization` on a change of origin and has no hook to add one: its B-75), and secrets are where an unreviewed TLS stack would cost most.
- **NET-1, part 3: the Go checksum database's answers checked as the go command checks them** (Oct 6; `rust/crates/lazaret-verify` (`gosum`: `tiles_needed` and `verify` over tiny_https's `sumdb::Check`, at most 64 tiles, a full tile standing in for a partial one), `rust/crates/lazaret-ffi` (`verify` module: the call `verify.go_sumdb`, native only, answered before the engine's calls; `lazaret-verify` a native-only dependency), `registry/ecosystems/golang.py` (`SUMDB_KEY`, the key Go pins; `verify_lookup`: the call twice, the tiles it names checked (paths, sizes, count) before any is fetched through the module's `Fetch`, a partial tile that is gone read from the full one; the hashes `parse_lookup` read must be lines of the signed record; `_Sumdb`: the newest head accepted is given to the next check, and tiles that passed are kept for the process (1,024) and checked again when used; `resolve` sets `info["sumdb"]` to "verified", or "tls" without a native library that checks); tests `tests/registry/test_golang_sumdb.py` (new: 13, on tiny_https's capture of the real `sum.golang.org`: the lookup verified alone and against a head kept from before, kept tiles read and checked again, a changed record, an unsigned head, each of the seven tiles changed or missing, a partial tile read from its full one, another key, hashes that are not the signed record's, tiles named that it cannot have, no native library, an older one, a failed check), lazaret-ffi's (2: the call on the capture, and what it refuses) and lazaret-verify's `gosum` (2); a fuzz target, `go-sumdb-check` (new: the real answer changed, its tiles served as captured: only FetchError, only tiles asked for, and an answer that is not refused is verified and says what the database signed; 27,496 runs, nothing found; `test_fuzz_scripts.py` breaks each promise); `test_golang.py` and `test_registry_go_crates.py` read their test-key lookups unchecked (`setUpModule`), as does the `go-resolve` fuzz target). An answer that does not check out is a FetchError: the module is not resolved. Not done: the newest head is not kept between runs (the go command keeps it in `$GOPATH/pkg/sumdb`), and `GOSUMDB`/`GONOSUMDB` are not read (the registry checks public modules against `sum.golang.org` only). The guard's Go relay is unchanged: go checks the database there itself.

- **GR-4: the Go guard's zips read and scanned within a byte budget** (Oct 6; `registry/guard.py` (`ByteGate`: at most a budget of bytes held at once by the threads that hold them, one larger than it alone, its peak kept; `Scanner.holding`, `hold_bytes()`, `LAZARET_GUARD_HOLD_MB`, default 512; `GoRelay._scan` and `_check_cached_zip` hold a zip's size around reading it and scanning it, the verdict cache asked first so a cached zip is not read); tests `test_guard_go.py` (`ByteGateTests`, 3; `ZipTests`: six zips asked for at once never held over the budget; the test scanner has the real gate)). go fetches about GOMAXPROCS zips at once; each was read whole and scanned in its request's thread, so sixteen 200 MiB modules could hold 3.2 GB. Measured with eight 40 MB zips asked for at once and a scan that holds each 0.3 s: 320 MB held at the peak with no budget (RSS up 326 MB), 80 MB with a budget of 100 MB (RSS up 92 MB), 1.3 s and 2.2 s. Left (done the same day, EG-4's leftover below): a manifest's twin (`Package.json` beside `package.json`), which Node and VS Code read from disk where the twin may have replaced it, was flagged and not read as the manifest by the scans (the guard refuses such a `.vsix`).
- **N-22, the scan pool's half: `close` waits for the workers it ended** (Oct 7; `registry/scanpool.py` (`_retire` returns the pool's processes and its manager thread as they were, since `shutdown` lets go of both; `close` waits for them, `CLOSE_WAIT` (5 s) at most, and kills a worker still there: `_reap`); tests `test_scanpool.py` (`close` leaves no worker and no manager thread alive; it fails on the old code)). Run under three CPU-burning processes on the sandbox's two cores, the module took 27 to 36 s with the old `close`, a pool's manager thread still alive after its last test, and 23 to 24 s with the new one, nothing left; the gates' parallel load had pushed the old one past the 45 s box (Oct 7, the GR-8 gates). `test_guard_yarn_bun`'s single pnpm failure (Oct 6) is not this. **Its half** (Oct 7): pnpm keeps packuments in `~/.cache/pnpm` by the registry's host and port, which every run shared, and the test registry's tarballs held the second they were made (gzip's header); a registry that got a port an earlier run's had was answered from that run's packument, with its tarball's digest, and the guard blocked the package, rightly (reproduced by binding the port again: "its sha512 digest is not the lockfile's"). `_guard_support.base_env` gives pnpm the run's own `cache-dir`, and `_review_support.tarball` makes the same bytes whenever it is called (tests `test_guard_yarn_bun.py`'s `IsolationTests`, which fail on the old code).
- **EG-5: the code an extension's contributions name is an entry point** (Oct 7; rule set 2.43.0; found by a second review of the extension model, which read VS Code's code (MIT) and TypeScript's (Apache-2.0) and ran a probe: a download-and-run, MAJOR, in a TypeScript server plugin or a debug adapter was OK, and a CRITICAL one there was SC-USE-RISK in the words of code the extension calls; `registry/repo.py` (`_vsix_contributions`: `typescriptServerPlugins` (a package's name only, as TypeScript takes it: `node_modules/<name>`, its own `package.json`'s `main` or its index), `debuggers` (`program` unless absolute, `runtime` when it begins `./`, in each platform's block too, as `ExecutableDebugAdapter.extract` joins them; JavaScript for a `.js` file or one `node` runs, Python for a `.py` one or one Python runs, `_debugger_lang`; one outside the folder is `_vsix_outside_issue` with the debug session's words), `notebookRenderer`'s `entrypoint` (a path or `{path}`) and `markdown.previewScripts`; `_vsix_special`, `_reach_of`, `_vsix_when`: the import-time finding of such a file, and of what it loads that `main` does not, says when it runs), `repo.ENGINE_VERSION` and the pack's `rule_set`; tests `test_vsix.py` (`ContributionTests`: a plugin and what it loads, a scoped one, names TypeScript does not load, a debug adapter's program, a platform's and a Python runtime, an absolute program, a runtime on the PATH, one outside the folder, the webview scripts, a file `main` loads too)). Tomcat: ";parameters" dropped); `covers`: a credential goes with a path whose directory is under its path read the three ways, CR-2 and CR-9; `header(url, from_url=…)`, `same_origin`: a redirect from another origin gets a credential of the whole host only, as npm sends none on such a redirect and pip a `.netrc` login, CR-9), `registry/guard.py` (the fetcher sends a request's URL and urllib's redirects with the path so read, and gives a redirect the credentials of its URL from where it came from; `Debug` of a request and a proxy shows no password, CR-7), `lazaret-ffi` (`"host"` in a tls-version failure); tests: `lazaret-net` 6 more (the choice of every path of two tables shared with `test_pmsettings.py`, on the request and on a redirect; a server without TLS 1.3 named by its hop; `Debug`), `test_pmsettings.py` (`RequestPathTests`: the "//" path answered, the shared tables, held equal to the Rust test's, the three readings, another origin, a backslash, a long path, a key with dot segments, `normal_path`), `test_guard.py` (a "//" path, dot segments and another origin on urllib's path, a host both transports read alike, a lockfile URL the guard reads differently from npm blocked), `test_guard_native_fetch.py` (the same paths on both transports, encoded ones too; Not changed here: tiny_https, a drop Lazaret does not edit; its next drop is asked for dot segments resolved in a Location's path whatever its form, a backslash refused or read as a slash, a port of digits only, a proxy's port read wherever its URL has a path, NO_PROXY read as urllib reads it, and no password in the `Debug` of a URL, a proxy or a pool's key.

- **EG-4's leftover: a file an installer opens by name is read under any case** (Oct 7; rule set 2.45.0; `registry/repo.py` (`OPENED_BY_NAME`, `opened_as`: `package.json`, `binding.gyp`, `pyproject.toml`, `setup.py` by their case fold; `_ArtifactScan.member` reads a member so named as that manifest; `_entry_point_manifests` reads a root manifest's case variant's entry points and implicit node-gyp hook, an extension's its `main`, `browser` and contributions; `_vsix_hook_words` its `vscode:uninstall`; `_python_install_scripts` a root `Setup.py` and every root `pyproject.toml` variant's in-tree backend); tests `test_review_archives.py` (`Package.json` after `package.json`: its hook CRITICAL, its `main`'s file imported; `PACKAGE.JSON` alone; `Setup.py` beside `setup.py`; a `PyProject.toml`'s backend), `test_vsix.py` (a twin's `main` and its `vscode:uninstall`); they fail on the old code). - **NET-1: provenance in the guard, measured first** (Oct 7; `registry/guard.py` (`PROVENANCE_DAYS` (30), `provenance_due`, `provenance_fetch`, `with_provenance`: a release from npm's or PyPI's public registry that the guard scans fresh and that was published less than 30 days ago, or whose publish time is not known, gets the registry's check, merged into the scan's verdict before it is cached (CRITICAL: SUSPICIOUS; MAJOR: WARN at least); `Check.provenance` in `--json`; in `check_npm_package`, `check_file` (uv.lock) and the local index's `scan` (pip, uv pip, uvx)), `registry/provenance.py` (`guard_npm`: npm's abbreviated document read once, for the version's `dist.attestations` and the release before it); tests `test_guard_provenance.py` (8: a release built like the one before, one without the provenance the one before had (WARN, blocked under `--block-warn`, cached with the verdict), attestations that do not hold (SUSPICIOUS, blocked), what is not checked (older than the window, another registry, a cached verdict, the switch), a document that cannot be read; PyPI from uv.lock and from the local index; the merge)). Measured first (`$SP/prov/`), on three npm projects resolved on Oct 7 (a web app, a tool chain, a server: 773 packages) and three Python sets (data, web, tools: 179 releases), with the registries' real answers: the whole check costs 1,140 requests and 136 MB for npm (774 abbreviated documents, 131 MB of them; `next`'s is over the 16 MB the check reads) and 313 requests and 80 MB for PyPI (the Simple API's JSON, 79 MB: `pydantic-core`'s is 9.4 MB), against the 641 MB of the npm projects' tarballs (every platform's); 29% of the npm releases and 39% of the PyPI ones have verified provenance; it flags two releases, both benign (pino 9.14.0 DROPPED after 9.13.1; annotated-doc 0.0.5 DROPPED after 0.0.4) and one same-owner change (scikit-learn 1.9.1, INFO). 127 of the npm releases and 57 of the PyPI ones are younger than 30 days: 311 requests and 67 MB, 122 and 47 MB, and neither benign drop. A real `lazaret guard --plan npm install` of the web project (58 packages for this machine) checks 19 (16 verified, `next` unchecked, 2 with none and nothing before them that had it), 99 more requests, no verdict moved, and no change in its wall time here (48.7 s, 50.3 s without). Decision 13 (WARN or SUSPICIOUS for a drop) is still John's: the guard takes the severity as built (WARN), so his answer moves both.
- **EG-7: the extension the editor installs in place of another** (Oct 7; found reading VS Code's code for `--update-extensions` (MIT): `getExtensionsControlManifest` turns the control list's `migrateToPreRelease` entries (for an engine the product takes) and the product's `defaultChatAgent` (VS Code's `GitHub.copilot`, replaced by `GitHub.copilot-chat`, a pre-release outside stable builds) into deprecations with `autoMigrate`, and `checkAndGetCompatibleVersion` installs the replacement, at its newest compatible version, for every gallery install: one asked for (after the editor finds it in the gallery), one an extension brings, an update; a `.vsix` is installed as itself. `registry/editorguard.py` (`read_control`: the malicious list and the replacements, read once, when first needed (`Run.malicious`, `Run.migrate`); `Run.replaced`; `_plan_roots`, `resolve_brought` (one Item for an extension however many ask for it at once: a lock per key), `walk` and `_update_choice` take the replacement; `_old_enough`: --min-age's hold-back, for an update or its replacement); tests `test_guard_editor.py` (`ReplacedTests`: what the list says, an install, a `.vsix`, what an extension brings, an update)).
- **The third review of the extension guard and of provenance in the guard: EG-8 to EG-18** (Oct 7; a fresh reader read the update and provenance work and EG-7 against VS Code's code (MIT) and the guard's, and found one serious item. **EG-8**: an editor whose `product.json` names a gallery the guard does not read, or none, was looked up in the public registry by name, and `--update-extensions` matched such an editor's extensions by name, so private names reached a public registry and a public extension of a private one's name was installed over it. `registry/editorguard.py` (`_gallery_config`: the editor's `product.json`'s `extensionsGallery`, and for VSCodium (`USER_GALLERY_EDITORS`) a `product.json` in its folder of user data and `VSCODE_GALLERY_SERVICE_URL`/`VSCODE_GALLERY_CONTROL_URL` over it, field by field, as VSCodium's `patches/00-settings-gallery.patch` and `docs/extensions.md` (MIT) read them; one that cannot be read: GuardError; `Editor.own_gallery` replaces `ids_comparable` (no `product.json` found: the editor's default is its own); `Editor.gallery_problem`: refused unless `--gallery` for an unread gallery or none, and `--update-extensions` only on the editor's own gallery; `_update_choice` always compares identifiers, and the `unmatched` path is gone). **EG-9** (rule set 2.46.0): `repo._resolve` tried every exact name before any case fold, so `node Setup` with `Setup.json` and `setup.js` read the JSON where macOS and Windows run `setup.js`; `_find_node` tries each candidate exactly, then by its fold, in Node's order, and a folder's `package.json` by its fold too (Python's imports stay exact). **EG-10**: a verdict was cached the same whether the provenance check had run to the end; `provenance.complete` (entries of answers that could not be read carry `retry`), the cache's `provenance` mark (`VerdictCache.put`), and `guard.provenance_owed` at the three cache lookups. **EG-17**: the guard's PyPI drop was the scanned file's; `check_pypi` records `releaseProvenance` (any file of the release listed with provenance) and `release_issues` uses it, as for the release before. **EG-18**: `PypiIndex.scan` checks provenance after the byte budget (the spooled file removed if blocked); a NUL in a profile location or a folder given is an error. **EG-11**: an extension the control list says is malicious is not replaced (`Run.listed`, `Run.replacement`), so `_check_malicious` blocks it as itself. **EG-12**: a truthy `deprecated` entry (JavaScript's truth, `_js_truthy`) removes `migrateToPreRelease`'s for its id. **EG-13**: `resolve_brought` keys by id alone; `walk` adds the replacement's id to what it knows and keeps one Item; `_plan_updates` keeps an extension's own update over another's replacement by it (`Item.replaces`). **EG-14**: no such version is `Unavailable(missing=True)`, so no replacement is looked for. **EG-15**: `_install` passes `--pre-release` for a pre-release follower (`Item.pre`), as VS Code's update installs it; the editor records the flag only if its command line waits for the lookup it starts after a file install (`InstallVSIXTask.updateMetadata`), so the rest is said in the docstring. **EG-16**: `Origin.everywhere` (`isApplicationScoped` or `isBuiltin`), `Item.everywhere`, and `_install` leaves `--profile` out for such an update, which VS Code's own update installs in the default profile (`abstractExtensionManagementService.installExtensions`); the editor would otherwise record it in the named profile, where it is filtered out. The review's third item, the signature check the editor does not make on a file, is decision 15. Tests: `test_guard_editor.py` (`GalleryTests`; `ForkVersionTests` with `--gallery`; an update with no `product.json` compares identifiers; a missing Marketplace version is not replaced; `ReplacedTests`' malicious original, `deprecated` over `migrateToPreRelease`, one install of a replacement brought twice, an own update over a replacement; the two install commands of a pre-release follower and of an extension in every profile; the profile record's scope; NUL locations), `test_review_archives.py` (Node's order under any case), `test_guard_provenance.py` (a verdict cached unchecked is checked again; the cache file's mark; a PyPI file without provenance in a release with it; the index's blocked file leaves the spool); each fails on the old code. The editor tests run with a home of their own, so a VSCodium on the machine running them is not read.)
- **T-1 and decision 9: the guard blocks a scan that could not read what a package can be made to hide** (Oct 7; John's answers to decisions 5 and 9; rule set 2.47.0). `registry/repo.py` (`INCOMPLETE_KINDS`: `time`, `work`, `code`, `archive`; `_ArtifactScan.truncate(…, kind)` and `cut` record why at each truncation: the deadline (`member`'s `time`, `out_of_time`), the engine (`scan_pending`'s engine truncations, `_unanswered`, `_phase`), code (a source file, a manifest or a reader's code over the size limit, `add_decode_issues`, `_text_of`'s dropped, oversize or unscanned file that runs unless its first bytes are a program's (`oversize_binary`), a hook command longer than is followed, a module's or a crate's code more than its reader holds, SC-UNREAD-CODE), the archive (`files`, `total`, `corrupt`); `scan_members`' result gains `incomplete`). `registry/guard.py` (`_scan_one` passes it on; `Scanner.scan` scans a time-out once more; `Scanner.remember` never caches one; `VerdictCache.put` keeps the kinds, so a hit blocks too; `Context.apply` blocks such an INCOMPLETE unless `--allow-incomplete`, `--trust` per package, `--block-warn` as before; `INCOMPLETE_SAID`). A download over the limit (`not_checked`'s TooLarge) and a program too large to read (the popular set's one INCOMPLETE, `@img/sharp-libvips-linuxmusl-x64`'s 16 MB library) go through as before. Tests `test_guard_incomplete.py` (each kind from a scan; the policy; the retry and the cache; the npm guard on a padded file and on the large library).
- **Decision 13: a dropped provenance blocks in the guard** (Oct 7; John: "Good with your finding let's do that"). `guard.with_provenance` marks the verdict `dropped` (SC-PROVENANCE-DROPPED), the cache keeps it, and `Context.provenance_block` blocks while the release is in the window (`provenance_due`), as pnpm's `trustPolicy: no-downgrade` fails an install; `--trust` lets one through; the verdict stays WARN, as reports give it. Tests `test_guard_provenance.py` (npm and PyPI blocked; `--trust`; a cached drop in and out of the window; the check off).
- **T-1's follow-up: bytecode that runs is code no one can read** (Oct 7; measured after T-1: the benchmark's one INCOMPLETE release, @builderius/ai-builder 1.0.3, runs a bytenode `.jsc` at install, and had no kind, so it went through). `repo._native_program` (a member whose first bytes are binary and a program's: `core.EXEC_MAGIC`, a PE's `MZ`) and `_ArtifactScan.native` replace `oversize_binary`: a binary or oversize file that runs is `code` unless it is a program (so @img/sharp-libvips' 16 MB library still goes through, and the benchmark's release now blocks). Of Part F's 4,104 Ubuntu Go modules, three are INCOMPLETE of a blocking kind and now block in `guard go` unless let through: Ubuntu's aws-sdk-go-v2 (23,304 entries in one module, the archive limit; the proxy serves it as many small modules), azure-sdk-for-go (513 million characters of code, the reader's limit) and go-git-fixtures (a 16 MB `data.go`). Tests `test_guard_incomplete.py` (a `.jsc` that runs; a text that starts `MZ=1;`).
- **BR-1: pyproject.toml read with a TOML reader on Python 3.10** (Oct 7; found by the efficiency and brittleness review, which reproduced it). `repo._pep517_backend` read `[build-system]` with a regex on 3.10 and missed the in-tree backend in six of seven forms TOML allows (a comment after the header, dotted keys, an inline table, quoted keys, an escape, a decoy section in a string): the backend pip runs got no install-script test there. It reads with `sca.load_toml` (tomllib from 3.11, the subset reader on 3.10), the regex only for a text neither reads. Test `test_review_entrypoints.py` (the seven forms with and without tomllib; failing on the old code).
